Non-Equilibrium Fluctuations for a Spatial Logistic Branching Process with Weak Competition Thanks: Department of Statistics, University of Oxford, 24 St Giles’, Oxford, United Kingdom OX1 3LB, Email: thomas.tendron@mail.mcgill.ca
Thomas Tendron

Abstract
. The spatial logistic branching process is a population dynamics model in which particles move on a lattice according to independent simple symmetric random walks, each particle splits into a random number of individuals at rate one, and pairs of particles at the same location compete at rate c c . We consider the weak competition regime c ↘ 0 c\searrow 0 , corresponding to a local carrying capacity tending to infinity like c − 1 c^{-1} . We show that the hydrodynamic limit of the spatial logistic branching process is given by the Fisher-Kolmogorov-Petrovsky-Piskunov equation. We then prove that its non-equilibrium fluctuations converge to a generalised Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process with deterministic but heterogeneous coefficients. The proofs rely on an adaptation of the method of v v -functions developed in [ Boldrighini et al., 1992 ] . An intermediate result of independent interest shows how the tail of the offspring distribution and the precise regime in which c ↘ 0 c\searrow 0 affect the convergence rate of the expected population size of the spatial logistic branching process to the hydrodynamic limit.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Particle system and rescaling

The spatial logistic branching process is a particle system which models the dynamics of a locally regulated population consisting of colonies that live on the discrete circle
\mathbb{Z}_{K}=\mathbb{Z}/K\mathbb{Z}
, for some integer
K\geq 2
. We focus on the system up to an arbitrary, but fixed, time horizon
T>0
. The individuals in the population reproduce asexually by multiple fission, migrate on the lattice, and compete for resources within their local colony. In the following definition, we give the precise instantaneous dynamics of the particle system.

Definition 1
.

(The spatial logistic branching process)

Let
{\mathbb{N}}=\{1,2,\cdots\}
denote the natural numbers, and fix a probability mass function
\mathrm{P}=(p_{\ell})_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and a constant
c>0
. We consider the particle system with the following instantaneous dynamics.

1.

Diffusion
: each particle in the system performs an independent simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}_{K}
.

2.

Branching
: independently of its movement, a particle splits into
\ell+1
particles at rate
p_{\ell}
. In particular, branching always increases the number of particles in the system.

3.

Competition
: each pair of particles at the same location coalesces at rate
c
.

Let
N_{t}(z)\geq 0
denote the number of particles at position
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}
at time
t\geq 0
. The spatial logistic branching process, or SLBP for short, is the strong Markov process
\mathrm{N}=((N_{t}(z))_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}}:t\geq 0)
. We call
\mathrm{P}
its offspring distribution.

Remark 2
.

We note that if the process is defined on
\mathbb{Z}
and
N_{0}
is not compactly supported, it is not immediately clear that the SLBP is well-defined for all times. In this article, we will restrict our attention to the SLBP with compactly supported initial data by working on
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}
.

We briefly mention two elementary properties of the SLBP. First, since
N_{0}:\mathbb{Z}_{K}\to{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
is finite and
\mu<\infty
, a simple comparison with a pure birth process shows that
\mathrm{N}
does not explode in finite time. Secondly, because of the local competition, the SLBP does not satisfy the branching property.

We are interested in a regime of weak competition where
c\searrow 0
. More precisely, we scale the process in the following way.

Definition 3
.

(Weak competition regime)

Fix an arbitrary
\kappa\geq 0
, and let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
denote a scaling parameter. The rescaled SLBP evolves on the discrete circle
\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{Z}_{K_{\varepsilon}}
, where
K_{\varepsilon}=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
. Consider
L:(0,1)\to{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
a function satisfying
\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}L(\varepsilon)=\infty
with
L(\varepsilon)=o(\varepsilon^{-\kappa})
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. Given an offspring distribution
\mathrm{P}=(p_{\ell})_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}
with finite mean
\mu>1
, we define its truncation
\mathrm{P}^{\varepsilon}=(p^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}
by

p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}=\begin{cases}c_{\varepsilon}p_{\ell},&\ell\leq L(\varepsilon)\\
0,&\ell>L(\varepsilon)\end{cases},\quad c_{\varepsilon}=\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L(\varepsilon)}p_{\ell}\right)^{-1}.

We consider the rescaling
\mathrm{N}^{\varepsilon}=((N^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z))_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}:t\geq 0)
of the SLBP with the following instantaneous dynamics.

1.

Diffusion
: each particle in the system performs an independent simple symmetric random walk on
\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
at the accelerated jump rate
\varepsilon^{-2}
.

2.

Branching
: independently of its movement, a particle splits into
\ell+1
particles at rate
p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}
- branching always increases the number of particles in the system.

3.

Weak competition
: each pair of particles at the same location coalesces at rate
\varepsilon^{\kappa}
. We call
\kappa
the competition exponent - the smaller
\kappa
is, the larger the coalescence rate
\varepsilon^{\kappa}
.

We call the process
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}=((X^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z))_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}:t\geq 0)
defined by
X^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z)=\varepsilon^{\kappa}N^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z)
, the rescaled SLBP.

Remark 4
.

In Theorems
5
and
9
below, we further rescale the lattice so that its mesh size is
\varepsilon
to obtain a diffusive rescaling of the random walks.

1.2.
Main results

The technical assumptions on the initial data
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
can only be stated after a number of definitions, so we postpone the detailed description of the initial data and we first present our results. Let us just mention that the initial data has the following property. For some
\rho_{0}:\mathbb{S}^{1}\to[0,2\mu]
and all small
\varepsilon>0
, the law of
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
is very close to a product over
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
of independent Poisson distributions with intensities
\varepsilon^{-\kappa}\rho_{0}(K_{\varepsilon}^{-1}z)
and rescaled by
\varepsilon^{\kappa}
. When
\varepsilon\searrow 0
,
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
converges in distribution to a product over
z\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
of Poisson distributions with intensities
\rho_{0}(z)
.

We define
\rho:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\to[0,2\mu]
the unique solution to the Cauchy problem for the Fisher-Kolmogorov-Petrovsky-Piskunov (FKPP) equation:

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho(t,z)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}\rho(t,z)+\rho(t,z)(\mu-\rho(t,z)/2),&t>0,\quad z\in\mathbb{S}^{1},\\
\lim_{t\searrow 0}\rho(t,z)=\rho_{0}(z),&\quad z\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.\end{cases}

Our first result shows the convergence in probability of the rescaled SLBP of Definition
3
to the
hydrodynamic limit

\rho
.

Theorem 5
.

(Weak law of large numbers)

Suppose that Assumption
A2
below holds and that the competition exponent satisfies
\kappa\in[0,3/8)
. Consider the action of the rescaled SLBP on test functions via

X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\varphi(\varepsilon z),\quad\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}),\quad t\in[0,T].

Let
\rho
act on test functions by

\rho_{t}(\varphi)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\rho_{t}(z)\varphi(z)dz,\quad\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}),\quad t\in[0,T].

Then, we have
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\to\rho
in probability in the Skorohod space
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. In particular, for every
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, we have
X^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\to\rho(\varphi)
in probability in the Skorohod topology on
\mathscr{D}([0,T],\mathbb{R})
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
.

Remark 6
.

In Theorem
5
, the precise upper bound
3/8
for the competition exponent
\kappa
is an artefact of the proof of the intermediate result Theorem
21
, and it is not indicative of special behaviour at that level of
\kappa
.

In our main result, the central limit theorem, we are interested in characterising the limiting non-equilibrium fluctuations of the rescaled SLBP as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. We introduce the fluctuations field in the next definition.

Definition 7
.

(Fluctuations field)

Let
\gamma\geq-1/2
and suppose that
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, satisfies the Assumption
A2
below. For all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we define

Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z)=\varepsilon^{\gamma-\kappa}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\mathbb{E}[X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)]),\quad z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon},\quad t\geq 0.

As we did in Theorem
5
for
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
, we may also view
\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}
as an element of
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
via its action

Y_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}Y_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\varphi(\varepsilon z),\quad\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}),\quad t\geq 0.

Remark 8
.

We may equivalently study the fluctuations process

Y_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)=\varepsilon^{\gamma-\kappa}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)),\quad z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon},\quad t\geq 0,

where
\rho^{\varepsilon}
is a suitable approximation to the solution
\rho
to the FKPP equation (
1.1
). The approximation is defined in (
1.10
) below, its convergence to
\rho
is studied in part one of Lemma
15
. In Theorem
21
, we show that
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z)-\mathbb{E}[X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)]=O(\varepsilon^{1+\kappa})
. To directly study the fluctuations of
X^{\varepsilon}
around the solution
\rho
to the FKPP equation, one would need to find the rate of convergence of
\rho^{\varepsilon}
to
\rho
.

In the study of the fluctations of the SLBP, we assume that the offspring distribution has a finite second moment.

Assumption A1
.

(Offspring distribution)

Recall that
\mathrm{P}=(p_{\ell})_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}
denotes the offspring distribution and that
\mu>1
is its (finite) mean. Assume that we have
\sigma^{2}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}(\ell-\mu)^{2}p_{\ell}<\infty
.

Our main result is the following central limit theorem.

Theorem 9
.

(Central limit theorem)

Suppose that Assumption
A1
, and Assumption
A2
below, both hold. Take
\gamma=(\kappa-1)/2
with
\kappa\in[0,3/8)
. Recall that
\rho
denotes the unique solution to the FKPP equation (
1.1
). Then
\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}\overset{d}{\to}\mathrm{Y}
in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
{\mathrm{Y}\in C([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})}
is the unique solution to the Cauchy problem

(1.2)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}Y=\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}+\mu-\rho\Big)Y+\sqrt{\rho}\partial_{z}\dot{W}^{1}+\sqrt{(\sigma^{2}+\mu^{2})\rho+\rho^{2}/2}\dot{W}^{2}&\textnormal{on }[0,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\\
Y_{t}\overset{d}{\to}Y_{0}\textnormal{ as }t\searrow 0,\end{cases},

where
\dot{W}^{1}
and
\dot{W}^{2}
are independent Gaussian space-time white noises on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and
Y_{0}
is a
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime}
-valued centred Gaussian variable with variance

\mathbb{E}[Y_{0}(\varphi)^{2}]=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\varphi(z)^{2}\rho_{0}(z)dz,\quad\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}).

Remark 10
.

1.

We note that the competition exponent
\kappa
of Definition
3
appears neither in the hydrodynamic limit (
1.1
), nor in the limiting fluctuations (
1.2
). Its contribution becomes clear in a result of independent interest stated in Theorem
21
below. In short, the competition exponent affects the convergence rate of the expected population size of the rescaled SLBP to its hydrodynamic limit. The larger
\kappa
, the weaker the competition, and the faster the convergence.

2.

The noise
\partial_{z}\dot{W}^{1}
results from fluctuations in the motion of particles, while the independent noise
\dot{W}^{2}
captures variations in the local density of particles due to the birth and competition mechanisms.

3.

The solution to (
1.2
) is unique in law and given by the mild solution (see e.g.
[
Walsh, 1986
, Chapter 5]
)

1.3.
A class of correlation functions: the
v
-functions

In establishing the limit theorems for the rescaled SLBP
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
, we study the behaviour of some correlation functions whose precise definition is adapted from
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
, and which are known as the
v
-functions. To state our assumptions on the initial data
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and
Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}
of Theorems
5
and
9
, we must first introduce the correlation functions. We consider the space
\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon^{\kappa}{\mathbb{N}}_{0})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}
of configurations of
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
.

Definition 11
.

We define a strict partial order
\preccurlyeq
on
\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
as follows. Given two configurations
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, we write
x\preccurlyeq x^{\prime}
if
x(z)\leq x^{\prime}(z)
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
. If
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
are such that
x\preccurlyeq x^{\prime}
, then we write
x\backslash x^{\prime}
for the configuration
y\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
which satisfies
y(z)=x(z)-x^{\prime}(z)
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
.

For a configuration
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, we define its support

\operatorname{supp}(x)=\{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}:x(z)>0\},

and its cardinality, i.e. the number of particle it contains, by

n_{x}=\varepsilon^{-\kappa}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}x(z).

If we are only interested in the number of particles at a single site
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, then we write
{n_{x}(z)=\varepsilon^{-\kappa}x(z)}
. By abuse of notation, we also think of a configuration
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
as an arbitrary but fixed enumeration
z^{x}_{1},\cdots,z_{n_{x}}^{x}
of its
n_{x}
particles. For any function
f:\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
, we introduce the notation

(1.3)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in x}f(z,x)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n_{x}}f(z^{x}_{i},x),

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in x}f(z,x)\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{n_{x}}f(z^{x}_{i},x).

A
v
-function is defined as the expectation of certain polynomials known in
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
as the
V
-functions, which we define now. In our work, we introduce a version of the
V
-functions which is rescaled in terms of
\varepsilon^{\kappa}
to capture the fact that, in the weak competition regime, the local equilibrium population size is of order
\varepsilon^{-\kappa}
. For
n,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, we let

(1.4)

Q_{k}(n)\coloneqq\begin{cases}n(n-1)\cdots(n-k+1)&1\leq k\leq n\\
1&k=0\\
0&k>n\end{cases},

denote the falling factorial. Given two configurations
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, we let

(1.5)

Q^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime})\coloneqq\prod_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(x)}\varepsilon^{\kappa n_{x}(z)}Q_{n_{x}(z)}(n_{x^{\prime}}(z)),

with
Q^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, where we use
0\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
to denote the configuration with no particles. In particular,
Q^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime})\geq 0
is non-zero if and only if
x\preccurlyeq x^{\prime}
.

Definition 12
.

(
V
-function)

Fix a function
\rho^{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
, and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Define

V^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime};\rho^{\varepsilon})\coloneqq\sum_{x_{0}\preccurlyeq x}Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},x^{\prime})(-1)^{n_{x}-n_{x_{0}}}\prod_{z\in x\backslash x_{0}}\rho^{\varepsilon}(z),\quad x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}.

If
n_{x}=n
for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, we say that the
V
-function is of order
n
.

The configuration
x
plays the role of an index or a test configuration (notice that the sum and product are taken over sub-configurations of
x
). Informally, we think of the
V
-function as a notion of distance between
x^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\varepsilon}
. For instance, if
x
consists of a single particle at
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, then one computes

(1.6)

V^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime};\rho^{\varepsilon})=x^{\prime}(z)-\rho^{\varepsilon}(z).

If
x
consists of two particles, one at
z_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, and one at
z_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\backslash\{z_{1}\}
, then

(1.7)

V^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime};\rho^{\varepsilon})=(x(z_{1})-\rho^{\varepsilon}(z_{1}))(x^{\prime}(z_{2})-\rho^{\varepsilon}(z_{2})),

which is also independent of
\varepsilon
. The diagonal terms are less intuitive: if
x
has two particles at the same location
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
and no other particles, then

(1.8)

V^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime};\rho^{\varepsilon})=(x^{\prime}(z)-\rho^{\varepsilon}(z))^{2}-\varepsilon^{\kappa}x^{\prime}(z).

Note that this time, the coefficients of the
V
-function depend on
\varepsilon
.

In the following definition, we introduce the correlation functions which are the central object of our study, and are adapted from
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
.

Definition 13
.

(
v
-function)

Fix a function
\rho^{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
, and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Given an initial measure
\nu^{\varepsilon}
on the configuration space
\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, we define the
v
-function
v^{\varepsilon}
by

v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,\rho^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\varepsilon}}[V^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon};\rho^{\varepsilon})],\quad x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon},\quad t\geq 0,

where
\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\varepsilon}}
denotes the expectation with
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}\sim\nu^{\varepsilon}
. If
n_{x}=n
for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, we say that the
v
-function is of order
n
. When working with
v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,\rho^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})
, we will sometimes use the notation
v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})
for simplicity. Additionally, if
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
is a deterministic starting configuration for the rescaled SLBP, we will write
v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,\rho^{\varepsilon}|X_{0}^{\varepsilon})
for
v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,\rho^{\varepsilon}|\delta_{X_{0}^{\varepsilon}})
.

As for the
V
-function, the argument
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
of a
v
-function is understood as a test configuration. If the test configuration
x
consists of a single particle at location
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,\rho^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})=\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\varepsilon}}[X^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z)]-\rho^{\varepsilon}.

We claim that, at a given time
t\geq 0
, the
v
-functions measure how close
X^{\varepsilon}_{t}
is to a family of independent Poisson random variables indexed by lattice sites
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
. This idea is formalised in the following lemma, whose proof can be found in Appendix
C.4
.

Lemma 14
.

Fix a function
\rho:\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
, and any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. We have
v^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})=0
for all test configurations
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
if and only if

(1.9)

\operatorname{Law}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon})=\bigotimes_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\operatorname{Law}(\varepsilon^{\kappa}\operatorname{Poi}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}\rho^{\varepsilon}(z))).

In Definition
13
, we choose
\rho^{\varepsilon}
close to the solution
\rho
to (
1.1
). Specifically, at a time
t\geq 0
, we will take
x^{\prime}=X_{t}^{\varepsilon}
the time-
t
configuration of the rescaled SLBP, and
\rho^{\varepsilon}=\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}
, where
\rho^{\varepsilon}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
is an approximation to the solution of the FKPP equation (
1.1
). Specifically, for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, given an initial function
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\to[0,2\mu_{\varepsilon}]
, we take
\rho^{\varepsilon}
to be the unique solution to the problem

(1.10)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)+\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)(\mu_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)/2),&z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\quad t>0,\\
\lim_{t\searrow 0}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)=\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z),&z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon},\end{cases}

where
\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}
denotes the discrete Laplacian which acts on functions
f:\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}f(z)\coloneqq\varepsilon^{-2}(f(z+1)+f(z-1)-2f(z)),\quad z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}.

In the following lemma, we recall some elementary properties of
\rho^{\varepsilon}
whose proofs can be found in Appendix
C.2
.

Lemma 15
.

The function
\rho^{\varepsilon}
defined by (
1.10
) has the following properties.

1.

Suppose that the initial data
\rho_{0}
in (
1.1
) is
C^{3}(\mathbb{S}^{1},[0,2\mu])
. Let
\widetilde{\rho}_{t}^{\>\varepsilon}(z)\coloneqq\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon}z)
for all
z=\pi(\widetilde{z})\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
with
\widetilde{z}\in[0,1]\cap K_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
t\geq 0
, where
\pi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
denotes the canonical projection. Extend
\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}
to all of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
by
C^{3}
interpolation. Then, we have
\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}\to\rho
uniformly on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, where
\rho
is the unique solution to (
1.1
).

2.

We have
\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}|\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z)|\leq\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}|\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}(z)|
, for all
0\leq t\leq T
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

Remark 16
.

In part one, a linear interpolation is sufficient for this lemma, but the proof of Theorem
9
(specifically Lemmas
51
and
53
in Appendix
C.3
) requires us to take derivatives up to order three to control discrete Laplacians.

1.4.
Assumptions on the initial data

In the following definition, we specify the general form of the initial data of the rescaled SLBP.

Definition 17
.

(Initial configuration of the rescaled SLBP)

Fix
\rho_{0}\in C^{3}(\mathbb{S}^{1},[0,2\mu])
. By considering the embedding
\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
given by
z\mapsto K_{\varepsilon}^{-1}z
, where we recall that
K_{\varepsilon}=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
, we define a function
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\to[0,2\mu]
by

(1.11)

\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)\coloneqq\rho_{0}(K_{\varepsilon}^{-1}z),\quad z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}.

Then, we assume that
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
the time-
0
configuration of the rescaled SLBP is distributed according to a probability measure
\nu^{\varepsilon}
on the configuration space
\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\varepsilon}}[X_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)]=\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon},

where the notation
\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\varepsilon}}
means that
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
is sampled from
\nu^{\varepsilon}
. Hereafter, we denote
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\varepsilon}}
. Additionally, we set the initial data
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}
in the Cauchy problem (
1.10
) for the semi-discrete FKPP equation using (
1.11
).

Remark 18
.

1.

For instance, one can take
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\equiv 2\mu_{\varepsilon}
, and at each site
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
an independent and identically distributed random variable
{X_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)\sim\varepsilon^{\kappa}\operatorname{Poi}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}2\mu_{\varepsilon})}
, where
\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)
denotes a Poisson random variable with intensity
\lambda>0
.

2.

The choice of
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}
fixes the initial relationship between the average starting configuration of the rescaled SLBP and the initial data of (
1.10
).

3.

Recall
\rho_{0}
and
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}
from Definition
17
. Since
\rho_{0}\in C^{3}(\mathbb{S}^{1},[0,2\mu])
, there is a
C^{3}
interpolation of the function
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon}\cdot)
,
z\in\pi([0,1]\cap K_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathbb{Z})
, such that its derivatives up to order three are bounded uniformly in
z\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. In particular, this implies that the spatial derivatives of
\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}
are uniformly bounded in space, time, and
\varepsilon
.

We have all the ingredients to state our assumptions on
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}
below.

Assumption A2
.

Consider
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
,
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and
\nu^{\varepsilon}
from Definition
17
, and fix any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We assume that the following conditions hold.

a.

There exists
c=c(n)>0
such that

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}v^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})\leq c\varepsilon^{\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor(1+\kappa)},

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Moreover,
c(n^{\prime})>c(n)
for all natural numbers
n^{\prime}>n
.

b.

There exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending only on
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}|\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)|
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[|X_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z_{1})-X_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z_{2})|^{2}\right]\leq c_{1}\varepsilon^{\kappa}+c_{2}|\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z_{1})-\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z_{2})|^{2},

for all
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

We make the following additional assumptions on the initial data
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}^{\>\varepsilon}
and
Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}
in our central limit theorem.

Remark 19
.

We are primarily interested in initial data
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
whose law is the product measure

(1.12)

\operatorname{Law}(X_{0}^{\varepsilon})=\bigotimes_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\operatorname{Law}(\varepsilon^{\kappa}\operatorname{Poi}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z))),

where
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}
was introduced in Definition
17
. With this assumption, one can check that if
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\equiv 2\mu_{\varepsilon}
in Definition
17
, then Assumption
A2
holds. As
2\mu=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}2\mu_{\varepsilon}
is a stable steady state of the FKPP equation (
1.1
), this corresponds to studying the equilibrium fluctuations of the rescaled SLBP in Theorem
9
. For the non-equilibrium fluctuations, we may choose
\rho_{0}\in C^{3}(\mathbb{S}^{1},[0,2\mu])
the initial data in (
1.1
) arbitrarily, and choose
\nu^{\varepsilon}
and
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}
as described in Definition
17
. Similarly, one easily checks that Assumption
A2
holds for the product initial law (
1.12
). Assumption
A2
only allows for vanishing correlations between sites as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
.

2.
Overview of the proofs and convergence of the
v
-functions

In this section, we outline the proof of Theorem
9
following the method of compactness-uniqueness (see
[
Billingsley, 1999
, Theorem 13.1]
). We show that the family
\{\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
is tight in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
, and that the finite-dimensional distributions of
\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}
converge as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
to those of
\mathrm{Y}
the solution to the stochastic partial differential equation (
5.1
). The main challenge is the following observation: the limiting fluctuations are characterised by the
linear
equation (
5.1
), but (
1.1
) the hydrodynamic equation is
non-linear
. Much of the work consists in justifying rigorously this linearisation. In particular, we must control the non-linear terms of the form
X^{\varepsilon}(z)^{2}
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, in the proof of tightness of
\{\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
, and we must show that they can be linearized in a suitable sense when computing the limit of the finite-dimensional distributions of
\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. Both of these obstacles are resolved by proving the following
Boltzmann-Gibbs principle
, and deriving some of its consequences.

Consider for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
a generalised test function

\varphi^{\varepsilon}:\{(r,t)\in[0,T]^{2}:0\leq r\leq t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{R},\quad((r,t),z)\mapsto\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(z),

which is assumed to be continuous in
r
for each fixed
t
and
z
, and such that
\varphi_{r,t}^{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
for all
r
and
t
with

C_{\varphi}=C_{\varphi}(T)\coloneqq\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{0\leq r\leq t\leq T}\|\varphi_{r,t}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}<\infty,

where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
denotes the supremum norm on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Let
T>0
,
\gamma\in[-1/2,-5/16)
, and
\kappa\in[0,1+2\gamma]
, and define the non-linear fluctuations field

F^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\varphi_{r,t}^{\varepsilon})\coloneqq\frac{\varepsilon^{1+\gamma-\kappa}}{2}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\left(X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)(X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa})-\mathbb{E}[X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)(X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa})]\right)\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon z),

for all
t\geq r
.

Proposition 20
.

(Boltzmann-Gibbs principle)

We have

(2.1)

\limsup_{S\to\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T}\mathbb{E}\left[\Bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}S}\int_{s}^{s+\varepsilon^{2}S}[F_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t})-Y_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{r}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t})]dr\Bigg)^{2}\right]=0.

We briefly give some intuition for this result. By Definition
3
, at a given location
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
and at time
t-
, births occur at rate
\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)
, a particle dies due to competition at rate
\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)(X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa})/2
, and a particle migrates to an adjacent site at rate
\varepsilon^{-2-\kappa}X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)
. The time integral in (
2.1
) is taken on the slow time scale
S\mapsto\varepsilon^{2}S
. On that time scale, the rates of birth, competition, and diffusion become
\varepsilon^{2-\kappa}X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)
,
\varepsilon^{2-\kappa}X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)(X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa})/2
, and
\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)
respectively. Since
\kappa\in[0,3/8]
by assumption in Theorem
9
, and
X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)
is of order one by Theorem
5
, the birth-death dynamics are essentially frozen, while we still see significant diffusion of particles. Diffusion conserves the total number of particles and helps the system reach its local equilibrium. The field
F^{\varepsilon}
captures the fluctuations of the process
(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon})^{2}
, while
\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}
is the fluctuations field of
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
. One easily checks that diffusion, which is the only remaining dynamics on the slow time scale, does not preserve particle numbers in
F^{\varepsilon}
. Thus the result says that we can project the fluctuations of the non-conserved quantity
(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon})^{2}
onto those of the conserved quantity
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
. It is helpful to recall that
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
and
\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}
both converge to the FKPP equation by Theorem
5
and Lemma
15
. This line of reasoning in particular describes the fluctuations of the system when
\kappa=0
(competition is not weak) and the branching is binary, which was studied in
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
.

The main ingredient in the proof of Proposition
20
is a quantitative convergence result for the
v
-functions (recall Definition
13
), which is of independent interest as it shows in particular the contributions of the tail of the offspring distribution and of the competition exponent
\kappa
to the rate of convergence of the
expected
population size of the rescaled SLBP to its hydrodynamic limit
\rho
.

Theorem 21
.

(Quantitative convergence of the
v
-functions)

Suppose that Assumption
A2
holds, that
\kappa\in[0,3/8)
, and that the offspring distribution
\mathrm{P}
has a finite moment of order
p
, for some
p\in[1,2]
. If
p\in[1,2]
and
n>2
, we have

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})|=o(\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}),\quad\textit{as $\varepsilon\searrow 0$}.

If
p\in[1,2)
and
n\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})|=o(\varepsilon^{1+(p-1)\kappa}),\quad\textit{as $\varepsilon\searrow 0$}.

If
p=2
and
n\in\{1,2\}
, there exists
c=c(T,p,n)>0
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})|\leq c\varepsilon^{1+\kappa},\quad\textit{for all $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$}.

In Assumption
A2
.a, we assume that the initial law of the rescaled SLBP is close to a product law over the sites in
\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
. Then by Lemma
14
, we can more generally interpret Theorem
21
as a result on the quantitative propagation of chaos for our population model. The
v
-functions vanish sufficiently fast to imply the Boltzmann-Gibbs principle claimed in Proposition
20
. Additionally, Theorem
21
allows us to show that the finite-dimensional distributions of the rescaled SLBP
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
converge to those of
\rho
the solution to (
1.1
). Then, we show using suitable martingale problems and Green’s function estimates that
\{X^{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
is tight in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
to obtain the weak law of large numbers, Theorem
5
.

The proofs of Proposition
20
and Theorem
21
follow closely those of Proposition 3.1 and Lemma 1 of
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
, respectively. We have adapted the authors’ method to our setting, which differs in the following two ways. The first difference concerns the rate of competition. In the work of Boldrighini et al., the competition rate does not depend on
\varepsilon
, which means in particular that the local number of particles does not tend to infinity when
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. In our weak competition regime, the coalescence rate is of order
\varepsilon^{\kappa}
, and the local number of particles tends to infinity when
\varepsilon\searrow 0
like
\varepsilon^{-\kappa}
. Thus, the law of large numbers claimed in Theorem 1 of
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
is only a consequence of the rescaling of the spatial motion, while our law of large numbers (Theorem
5
) results from the joint rescaling of space and of the local population size. This observation is the reason that our
v
-functions in Definition
13
are rescaled in terms of
\varepsilon^{\kappa}
. One recovers the
v
-functions of Boldrighini et al. if the competition exponent is
\kappa=0
.

The second substantial change has to do with the offspring distribution. Boldrighini et al. consider binary branching only, while in the context of the law of large numbers, we allow any offspring distribution with finite mean. To follow the method of proof of Lemma 1 from
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
then requires us to suitably truncate the offspring distribution in terms of
\varepsilon
so as to control its truncated higher order moments, and to adapt arguments which use the symmetry present in Boldrighini et al. where birth and death events respectively add or remove a single particle locally, to our asymmetric local birth-competition process where birth events add any number of offspring while competition still only removes one particle per event. In particular, we adapt a key component of the proof - the fact that the
v
-functions satisfy a hierarchy of ordinary differential equations with respect to time with dynamics that are dual in some sense to the dynamics of the original particle system. See Proposition
22
below. In
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
, this hierarchy relates the
v
-function of order
n
to those of orders
k\in\{n-2,n-1,n,n+1\}
(assuming linear birth rate and quadratic competition rate as in the present paper), while in our case, the hierarchy relates an
n
-th order
v
-function to the
v
-functions of orders
k\in\{1,2,\cdots,n,n+1\}
. This change reflects the fact that in the hierarchy for general offspring distributions, during a single birth-competition event, at most one particle is added to the system locally, while the entire local population can be whipped out. As a consequence, additional care is required when controlling transition probabilities in Lemma
39
below. Let us state the hierarchy of equations for the
v
-functions.

Proposition 22
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and recall that
\rho^{\varepsilon}
denotes the solution to the semi-discrete FKPP equation (
1.10
). Let
\nu^{\varepsilon}
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
such that for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and all
t\geq 0
, we have

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\varepsilon}}[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X^{\varepsilon}_{t})]<\infty.

Then

(2.2)

\displaystyle v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x_{1})v_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1},\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}ds\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x_{1})\sum_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(x_{1})}\sum_{h=-n_{x_{1}}(z)}^{1}c_{h}^{\varepsilon}(n_{x_{1}}(z),\rho_{s}^{\varepsilon}(z))v_{s}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1}^{(z,h)},\rho_{s}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon}),

for all
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
and
t\in[0,T]
, for some coefficients
c^{\varepsilon}_{h}(m,u)
, and where
x_{1}^{(z,h)}
denotes the configuration
x_{1}
with
h
particles added at
z
if
h\geq 0
, and
-h
particles removed from
z
if
h<0
. Moreover, there exists a constant
c=c(n)>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that

(2.3)

|c^{\varepsilon}_{h}(m,u)|\leq c\max(1,|u|^{m+1}),\quad m\in\{1,\cdots,n\},\quad u\in\mathbb{R},\quad h\in\{-m,\cdots,1\},\quad\varepsilon\in(0,1).

In the particular case where
m=2
and
h=-2
and the offspring distribution
\mathrm{P}
has a moment of order
p\in[1,2]
, there also exists
c>0
such that

(2.4)

|c_{-2}^{\varepsilon}(2,u)|\leq c\varepsilon^{\kappa}\max(1,|u|^{3})+o(\varepsilon^{(p-1)\kappa})|u|,

as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. For
m=2
and
h=-1
, we have

(2.5)

c_{-1}^{\varepsilon}(2,u)=(2\mu_{\varepsilon}+1)\varepsilon^{\kappa}+o(\varepsilon^{(p-1)\kappa}),

as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. Finally, if
m=1
and
h\leq-1
, we have
c_{h}^{\varepsilon}(1,u)=0
.

For low order
v
-functions, the coefficients in the hierarchy are small in
\varepsilon^{\kappa}
. This is the main reason why the convergence rates in Theorem
21
have factors involving
\varepsilon^{\kappa}
, and it provides the necessary control for the proof of the Boltzmann-Gibbs principle of Proposition
20
.

3.
Motivation and related works

In this section, we discuss our motivation for the study of the SLBP (Definition
1
) and for the weak competition rescaling (Definition
3
). We mention some closely-related models from the literature.

[
Fournier and Méléard, 2004
]
study a spatial population process in continuous time and continuous space with the application to the modeling of plant populations in mind. In their model, the plants have an opportunity to move (disperse) at the time of their birth, and they subsequently are motionless. They die at some rate due to intrinsic reasons or due to competition with neighbouring plants. At any given point in time, the system is characterized by the locations of the plants in the closure of an open and connected subset
\chi
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for some
d\geq 1
. More specifically, the dynamics are as follows. Each plant at location
x\in\bar{\chi}
at time
t\geq 0

1.

dies at rate
\mu(x)\in[0,\infty)
,

2.

produces a seed at rate
\gamma(x)\in[0,\infty)
, which appears instantaneously as a mature plant at a location
x+z
, where
z
sampled from a dispersion measure
D(x,dz)
supported on
\bar{\chi}-x
, and

3.

dies due to non-local competition at rate
\alpha(x)\sum_{i=1}^{I(t)}U(x,X^{i}_{t})\in[0,\infty)
, where
{U(x,y)=U(y,x)}
is a competition kernel,
I(t)
is the total number of plants alive in the system at time
t
and
(X^{i}_{t})_{1\leq i\leq I(t)}
are the positions of all the plants.

The authors track the current state of the population via its empirical measure

\nu_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{I(t)}\delta_{X^{i}_{t}}(dx),\quad t>0.

They call this process the
Bolker–Pacala–Dieckmann–Law (BPDL) process
, in reference to the ecology literature where it originated (
[
Law and Dieckmann, 1998
]
and
[
Bolker and Pacala, 1999
]
). In Theorem 5.3, they establish the following hydrodynamic limit. For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
(\nu_{t}^{n})_{t\geq 0}
denote the BPDL process with the same parameters as above, except for the competition strength
\alpha(x)
which is scaled as follows:
\alpha_{n}(x)\coloneqq\alpha(x)/n
. Define the process
\xi_{t}^{n}=\frac{1}{n}\nu_{t}^{n}
,
t>0
. The scaling of the competition strength is analogous to the weak competition regime that we study in this paper for the SLBP. In
[
Bansaye and Méléard, 2015
, Section 7]
, the authors suggest the following biological interpretation of this type of rescaling. They interpret the parameter
n
(or
\varepsilon^{-\kappa}
for the SLBP) as the amount of resources available in the system. If a large amount of resources is available, there is less need for individuals in the population to compete for resources. Additionally, the partition of a fixed amount of resources among individuals in the population may force the biomass of each individual to be inversely proportional to the amount of resources available. Fournier and Méléard show that
(\xi^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converges in distribution in a suitable path space as
n\to\infty
to the solution to the Cauchy problem for the integro-differential equation

(3.1)

\partial_{t}\xi_{t}(x)=\int_{\bar{\chi}}dy\xi_{t}(y)\gamma(y)D(y,x-y)-\xi_{t}(x)\left(\mu(x)+\alpha(x)\int_{\bar{\chi}}dy\xi_{t}(y)U(x,y)\right),

where
x\in\bar{\chi}
and
t>0
. The BPDL defers from the SLBP in two major ways. First, since the competition is modelled using an interaction kernel summed over the entire population, it is at least from the mathematical point of view
non-local
. This is also reflected in the hydrodynamic limit (
3.1
). Secondly, by
[
Fournier and Méléard, 2004
, Proposition 3.2]
, if at the time zero, there is at most one plant at each location, then this holds true for all future times. By contrast, the interactions in the SLBP are completely local, and the population size on any given site is unbounded. Both of these features are of interest for the modelling of locally-regulated populations where one can reasonably assume at the microscopic level that interactions occur within large colonies of individuals at the same location and individuals can migrate to adjacent colonies. The methods of proof used in
[
Fournier and Méléard, 2004
]
do not directly apply to our particle systems as they are suitable for non-local competition dynamics.

In
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
, the authors prove limit theorems for the SLBP with fixed amount of resources (
\kappa=0
), and with binary branching. More precisely, they consider the SLBP
\mathrm{N}=((N_{t}(z))_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{K}}:t\geq 0)
in Definition
1
(without the
1/2
in the rate of competition). They rescale the spatial motion diffusively by working on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}_{K_{\varepsilon}}
where
K_{\varepsilon}=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-2}\rfloor
, by accelerating the rate of migration by
\varepsilon^{-2}
, and by letting the resulting process
\mathrm{N}^{\varepsilon}=((N^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z))_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}:t\geq 0)
act on Schwartz test functions
\varphi\in{\mathscr{S}}(\mathbb{R})
via

X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\coloneqq\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}N_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\varphi(\varepsilon z),\quad t>0.

They use a growing discrete circle to obtain equations on all of
\mathbb{R}
as opposed to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
in our case. We find that the basic Green function estimates (Appendix
C.1
) used to obtain tightness of
\{X^{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
for some fixed time horizon
T>0
do not allow one to work on the growing circle, hence our choice of
K_{\varepsilon}=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
. Boldrighini et al. sketch a proof that
X^{\varepsilon}
converges in distribution in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
to the unique solution to the FKPP equation

(3.2)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho(t,z)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}\rho(t,z)+\rho(t,z)(1-\rho(t,z)),&t>0,\quad z\in\mathbb{R},\\
\lim_{t\searrow 0}\rho(t,z)=\rho_{0}(z),&\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

They then show that the non-equilibrium fluctuations of
N^{\varepsilon}
, understood as

Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\varphi)\coloneqq\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\left(N_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\mathbb{E}\left[N_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\right]\right)\varphi(\varepsilon z),\quad\varphi\in{\mathscr{S}}(\mathbb{R}),\quad t\geq 0,

converge in distribution in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],{\mathscr{S}}(\mathbb{R})^{\prime})
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
to the solution to the Cauchy problem

(3.3)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}Y=\Big(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}+1-2\rho\Big)Y+\sqrt{\rho}\partial_{z}\dot{W}^{1}+\sqrt{\rho+\rho^{2}}\dot{W}^{2}&\textnormal{on }[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\\
Y_{t}\overset{d}{\to}Y_{0}\textnormal{ as }t\searrow 0,\end{cases},

where
\dot{W}^{1}
and
\dot{W}^{2}
are independent Gaussian space-time white noises on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, and
Y_{0}
is a
{\mathscr{S}}(\mathbb{R})^{\prime}
-valued centred Gaussian variable with variance

\mathbb{E}[Y_{0}(\varphi)^{2}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi(z)^{2}\rho_{0}(z)dz,\quad\varphi\in{\mathscr{S}}(\mathbb{R}).

The proof is based on a Boltzmann-Gibbs principle analogous to (
2.1
) which results from the study of the asymptotics of some
v
-functions. The
v
-functions are defined, for all
x\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}
and
t\geq 0
, by

v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\varepsilon}}\left[\sum_{x_{0}\preccurlyeq x}\prod_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(x)}Q_{x(z)}(N^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z))(-1)^{n_{x}-n_{x_{0}}}\prod_{z\in x\backslash x_{0}}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\right],

where
Q_{\cdot}(\cdot)
is the falling factorial in (
1.4
),
\rho^{\varepsilon}
approximates the solution
\rho
to (
3.2
), and
\nu^{\varepsilon}
is a suitable initial law (this formula can be recovered from Definition
13
by taking
\kappa=0
). The authors prove that for all
n>2
, we have

(3.4)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})|=o(\varepsilon),\quad\textit{as $\varepsilon\searrow 0$},

and there exists
c=c(T)>0
such that

(3.5)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})|\leq c\varepsilon,\quad\textit{for all $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$},

for
n\in\{1,2\}
. As we discussed for Theorem
21
in the previous section, this result can be interpreted as the quantitative propagation of chaos for the SLBP with
\kappa=0
. Interestingly, we observe by comparing the hydrodynamic limit (
1.1
) with (
3.2
), and the limiting Ornstein-Ulhenbeck process (
1.2
) with (
3.3
) that the parameter
\kappa
does not play a role in the macroscopic dynamics. The difference between
\kappa=0
and
\kappa>0
is only apparent in the time-
t
measurement of propagation of chaos, that is the convergence rate of the
v
-functions. Indeed, comparing Theorem
21
with (
3.4
) and (
3.5
), we see that assuming
\kappa>0
(weak competition) and an offspring distribution with a moment
p>1
, the local densities of the SLBP at two distinct sites are guaranteed to become independent faster as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, than if
\kappa=0
. To tie this back to the interpretation of
[
Bansaye and Méléard, 2015
]
recalled above, in a system with more resources and more individuals with lower biomass, the behaviour of individuals at different sites decouples faster. On the qualitative side, we observe that when
\kappa=0
, the limit theorems of
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
are only a consequence of the diffusive rescaling of the random walks. This differs from our results for
\kappa>0
, where birth-competition dynamics are also rescaled. Finally, we note that
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
allow more general polynomial rates of birth and death, as long as the degree of the death rate is strictly larger than that of the birth rate.

[
Doering et al., 2003
, pages 2-4]
briefly discuss a mean-field approximation to the rescaled SLBP. Consider a birth and death process where each particle splits into two particles independently at rate
1
, and pairs of particles coalesce at rate
\chi
. Let
N(t)
denote the number of particles in the system. Equating the rates of birth and death and solving for
N
, we see that
\overline{N}=1+2\chi^{-1}
is a natural equilibrium population size, which we interpret as the carrying capacity of the system. Then, to first order, we have that
U(t)=N(t)/\overline{N}
satisfies the logistic equation
U^{\prime}(t)=U(t)(1-U(t))
. See for instance section 3.1 of
[
Bansaye and Méléard, 2015
]
for rigorous arguments. To take a scaling limit of large population size, Doering et al, consider the weak competition regime in which
\chi\searrow 0
, in our notation
\chi=\varepsilon^{\kappa}
and
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. For this process,
[
Doering et al., 2003
]
write that they expect the magnitude of the fluctuations to be of order
\sqrt{U+aU^{2}}
for some
a>0
. This is confirmed for the spatial process itself as a particular case of our main result, Theorem
9
. More generally, our result identifies the constants in terms of the first and second moments of the offspring distribution
\mathrm{P}
.

Before closing this section, let use cite other relevant works on scaling limits of regulated populations, and on Boltzmann-Gibbs principles. Local-regulation in the SLBP is endogenous. This contrasts with models such as the
N
-branching Brownian motions ( for instance
[
McKean, 1975
]
,
[
Brunet et al., 2006
]
,
[
Brunet et al., 2007
]
,
[
Berestycki and Zhao, 2018
]
, and
[
Berestycki et al., 2022
]
) and the stepping stone models (e.g.
[
Shiga and Uchiyama, 1986
]
,
[
Renshaw, 1986
]
,
[
Shiga, 1988
]
, and
[
Etheridge, 2004
]
), in which the global, respectively local, population size is fixed exogenously and constant over time.
[
Müller and Tribe, 1995
]
,
[
Forien and Penington, 2017
]
,
[
Etheridge et al., 2020
]
and
[
Bodineau et al., 2020
]
have obtained similar scaling limits for various interacting particle systems.
[
Fournier and Méléard, 2004
]
also studied convergence of their population model to a superprocess version of a model of
[
Bolker and Pacala, 1999
]
introduced by
[
Etheridge, 2004
]
, and which is a spatial analogue of the logistic Feller diffusion. In works on fluctuations of particle systems, e.g.
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
,
[
Chen and Fan, 2016
]
,
[
Forien and Penington, 2017
]
,
[
Labbé, 2018
]
and
[
Bodineau et al., 2020
]
, the reaction term in the stochastic partial differential equation which characterises the limiting fluctuations corresponds to a linearization of the reaction term in the partial differential equation describing the hydrodynamic limit. We also observe this phenomenon in our work: see Theorems
5
and
9
. To prove this relationship rigorously, one approach consists in showing a Boltzmann-Gibbs principle such as Proposition
20
. This principle was originally proved in
[
Brox and Rost, 1984
]
to study the equilibrium fluctuations of the zero range process. Broadly speaking, it states that the fluctuations of local functions of conserved quantities can be suitably projected onto the fluctuations of the conserved quantities. The Boltzmann-Gibbs principle continues to be used in the study of equilibrium fluctuations of particle systems; see e.g.
[
Labbé, 2018
]
and
[
Hernández et al., 2017
]
for recent examples. It was also shown to hold in some non-equilibrium settings: see for instance
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
,
[
Chen and Fan, 2016
]
, and the present work. In these examples, the intuition for the validity of the principle is similar to our heuristics below Proposition
20
in the previous section.

4.
Proof of the law of large numbers

In this section, we prove the law of large numbers stated in Theorem
5
, assuming the convergence of the
v
-functions claimed in Theorem
21
and proved in Section
6
below. Theorem
5
states that
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\overset{d}{\to}\rho
in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, where
\rho
solves (
1.1
):

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho(t,z)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}\rho(t,z)+\rho(t,z)(\mu-\rho(t,z)/2),&t>0,\quad z\in\mathbb{S}^{1},\\
\lim_{t\searrow 0}\rho(t,z)=\rho_{0}(z),&\quad z\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.\end{cases}

By
[
Billingsley, 1999
, Theorem 13.1]
- this result follows from the tightness of
\{\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
and from the convergence
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\to\rho
in finite-dimensional distributions as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. We show the convergence of the finite-dimensional distributions in Proposition
23
below using the asymptotics established in Theorem
21
for the
v
-functions. By Mitoma’s Theorem (see Theorem 4.1 in
[
Mitoma, 1983
]
),
\{\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
is tight in the Skorohod J1-topology on
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
if, for every
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, the family of processes
\{\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi):\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
is tight in the Skorohod J1-topology on
\mathscr{D}([0,T],\mathbb{R})
. By Aldous’ Criterion
[
Billingsley, 1999
, Theorem 16.10 and equation (16.32)]
, the later condition holds if the following two points are true.

1.

For each
t\in[0,T]
,
\{X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi):\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
is tight in
\mathbb{R}
, i.e. for any
\eta\in(0,1)
, there exists
K=K(t,\eta)>0
such that

(4.1)

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\mathbb{P}(|X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)|>K)<\eta.

2.

Given a family
\{\tau_{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
of
[0,T]
-valued stopping times and any family
\{\theta_{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}\subset[0,\infty)
with
\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\theta_{\varepsilon}=0
, we have

(4.2)

\limsup_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}\left[|X_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-X_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)|\right]=0.

The first condition (
4.1
) follows immediately from the convergence of the finite-dimensional distributions of
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
. The proof of (
4.2
) utilises martingale problems, Assumptions
A2
, Green’s function estimates shown in Lemma
49
in Appendix
C.1
, and Lemma
15
to obtain uniform equicontinuity estimates for the expectation in (
4.2
). We note that since our limit (
7
)-(
1.1
) is deterministic, the full convergence will also hold in probability.

4.1.
Convergence of finite-dimensional distributions

We establish the following proposition.

Proposition 23
.

Let
\rho
denote the solution to the FKPP equation (
1.1
) and define

\rho_{t}(\varphi)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\rho_{t}(z)\varphi(z)dz,\quad\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}),\quad t\in[0,T].

Then, for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}\in[0,T]
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, we have

(X_{t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi),\cdots,X_{t_{k}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))\overset{d}{\to}(\rho_{t_{1}}(\varphi),\cdots,\rho_{t_{k}}(\varphi)),

as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
.

Proof.

Define

\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\coloneqq\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\varphi(\varepsilon z),\quad\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}),\quad t\in[0,T],

and let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded Lipschitz function. By a Taylor expansion of order one, we have

\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))]=f(\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))+\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))f^{\prime}(\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)+\xi)],

for some
\xi\in(0,X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))
. We aim to control the second term using Theorem
21
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
\|f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}<\infty
, we obtain

(4.3)

\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))f^{\prime}(\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)+\xi)]\leq\|f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))^{2}]^{1/2}

To use the convergence of the
v
-functions, we need to rewrite the integrand in terms of pointwise differences of
X^{\varepsilon}_{t}
and
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}
. By definition, we have

(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))^{2}=\left(\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z))\varphi(\varepsilon z)\right)^{2}.

By the Jensen and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities,

(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))^{2}\leq\left(\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z))^{2}\right)\left(\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\varphi(\varepsilon z)^{2}\right).

Using (
1.8
), we see that

(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z))^{2}=V^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon};\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon})+2\varepsilon^{\kappa}X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z),

where
x=2\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\}}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
denotes the configuration with two particles at
z
.
Gathering the above estimates shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\varphi(\varepsilon z)^{2}\right)\times\left[\max_{x=2\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\}}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}}\left(v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})+2\varepsilon^{\kappa}\mathbb{E}[X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)]|\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)|\right)\right].

By Theorem
21
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\max_{x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}:n_{x}=2}v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})=0.

Since
X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)=Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon})
if
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
denotes the configuration with a single particle at
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, then we infer from Proposition
24
that

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)]<c(T),

for some absolute constant
c(T)>0
. By part two of Lemma
15
, we have
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}<\infty
. This shows that

\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))^{2}]=0.

Finally, recall from part one of Lemma
15
that as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, we have that
\widetilde{\rho}_{t}^{\>\varepsilon}(z)\coloneqq\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon}z)
,
z\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, converges uniformly on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
to the solution
\rho
of the FKPP equation (
1.1
). Therefore, writing
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)=\widetilde{\rho}_{t}^{\>\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon}^{-1}z)
, we compute

\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\varphi(\varepsilon z)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\widetilde{\rho}_{t}^{\>\varepsilon}(K_{\varepsilon}^{-1}z)\varphi(\varepsilon z)

\displaystyle\overset{\varepsilon\searrow 0}{\to}\rho_{t}(\varphi)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\rho_{t}(z)\varphi(z)dz,

where we have used that
K_{\varepsilon}^{-1}z\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
for
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, and
\varepsilon K_{\varepsilon}\to 1
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. Thus, by continuity of
f
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}f(\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))=f(\rho_{t}(\varphi)).

Overall, we have shown that

\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))]=f(\rho_{t}(\varphi)),

for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and bounded Lipschitz
f
. One can easily extend this reasoning to a finite number of times
t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}\in[0,T]
to obtain

\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi),\cdots,X_{t_{k}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))]=f(\rho_{t_{1}}(\varphi),\cdots,\rho_{t_{k}}(\varphi)),

for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and
k
-dimensional bounded Lipschitz
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, by a Taylor expansion of order one, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f(X_{t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi),\cdots,X_{t_{k}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))]

\displaystyle=f(\rho_{t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi),\cdots,\rho_{t_{k}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{t_{i}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\rho_{t_{i}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))R_{1}(X_{t_{i}^{\varepsilon}}(\varphi))\right],

where

|R_{1}(X_{t_{i}^{\varepsilon}}(\varphi))|\leq\max_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:|\xi|\leq|X_{t_{i}^{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)-\rho_{t_{i}^{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)|}|\nabla f(\xi)|\leq\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}|\nabla f(\xi)|<\infty

for each
i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
. Then we may apply the arguments starting from (
4.3
) above for each
i
separately.
∎

4.2.
Moments and martingale problems

The aim of this section is to prove the following moment control.

Proposition 24
.

For any
T>0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
c=c(T,n)>0
such that

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X^{\varepsilon}_{t})\right]\leq c.

To prove this proposition, we follow the strategy used to prove Proposition 2.1 in
[
Boldrighini et al., 1987
]
. Namely, we apply Dynkin’s formula to the test functions
\mathrm{X}\mapsto Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X^{\varepsilon}_{t})
. Then we upper bound the expectation of the birth-competition component of the generator, and we obtain a recursive inequality in
n_{x}
which we can bound by iteration.

Before proceeding with this agenda, we compute the generator of the rescaled SLBP
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}=(\{X^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z),\;z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\})_{t\geq 0}
. Let us recall its instantaneous dynamics. Define two polynomials
q_{+}^{\varepsilon}(y)=\varepsilon^{-\kappa}y
and
q_{-}^{\varepsilon}(y)=\varepsilon^{-\kappa}y(y-\varepsilon^{\kappa})/2
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

1.

Spatial motion: particles perform independent simple symmetric random walks on
\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
at rate
\varepsilon^{-2}
.

2.

Local births: independently at every site
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, we have at time
t-
a transition
X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)\to X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)+\varepsilon^{\kappa}\ell
at rate
q_{+}^{\varepsilon}(X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z))p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}
, for all
\ell\geq 1
.

3.

Local competition: independently at every site
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, we have at time
t-
a transition
X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)\to X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa}
at rate
q_{-}^{\varepsilon}(X_{t-}^{\varepsilon}(z))
.

In view of these dynamics, the generator
\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}
of
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
can be decomposed as the sum of a spatial motion component
\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{RW}
and a birth-competition component
\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}
. Given a test function
f:\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

(4.4)

\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}f(x)=\mathscr{G}_{RW}^{\varepsilon}f(x)+\mathscr{G}_{BC}^{\varepsilon}f(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}.

where

(4.5)

\mathscr{G}_{RW}^{\varepsilon}f(x)=\frac{\varepsilon^{-2}}{2}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\varepsilon^{-\kappa}x(z)\Big\{f(x^{z,z+1})+f(x^{z,z-1})-2f(x)\Big\},

with

x^{z,z\pm 1}(z^{\prime})=\begin{cases}x(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa},&z^{\prime}=z\\
x(z)+\varepsilon^{\kappa},&z^{\prime}=z\pm 1\\
x(z^{\prime}),&z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\backslash\{z,z\pm 1\},\end{cases}

and

(4.6)

\mathscr{G}_{BC}^{\varepsilon}f(x)=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\Big\{\sum_{\ell\geq 1}p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}q_{+}^{\varepsilon}(x(z))(f(x^{z,\ell})-f(x))+q_{-}^{\varepsilon}(x(z))(f(x^{z,-1})-f(x))\Big\}.

with

(4.7)

x^{z,\ell}(z^{\prime})=\begin{cases}x(z)+\ell\varepsilon^{\kappa},&z^{\prime}=z\\
x(z^{\prime}),&z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\backslash\{z\}\end{cases},\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{-1\}.

We introduce the notation
\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})
,
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
n_{x}=n_{x^{\prime}}
, for the probability of transition between configurations
x
and
x^{\prime}
in time
t
, purely from spatial motion. It is clear that

(4.8)

\mathbb{G}_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{n_{x}!}\sum_{\sigma}\prod_{z\in x}G_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z,\sigma(z)),

where
\sigma
runs over the set of bijections
x\to x^{\prime}
. If
x=x^{\prime}=0
, we set the sum to zero by convention. Observe that
\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
is symmetric in its arguments. We introduce the discrete Laplacian acting on functions
F:\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
:

(4.9)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}F(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\varepsilon^{-2}(F(x^{z,z+1})+F(x^{z,z-1})-2F(x)),\quad z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\Delta^{\varepsilon}F(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{z\in x}\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}F(x).

for all
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, where the updated configurations
x^{z,z\pm 1}
are defined in (
4.7
) and the summation over
z\in x
is defined in (
1.3
). We use the same notation for the Laplacian acting on
f:\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
:

(4.10)

\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}f(z)=\varepsilon^{-2}(f(z+1)+f(z-1)-2f(z)),\quad z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}.

In the following lemma, we derive a martingale problem for the rescaled SLBP.

Lemma 25
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. For any
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, the process
\mathrm{M}^{\varepsilon}(x)=(M^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x))_{t\geq 0}
defined by

(4.11)

M^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x)=Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X^{\varepsilon}_{t})-\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X^{\varepsilon}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x^{\prime})\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})ds,\quad t\geq 0,

where
x^{\prime}
ranges over configurations
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
n_{x^{\prime}}=n_{x}
, is a martingale.

Proof.

To lighten the notation, we introduce the notation
F(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s})=\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x^{\prime})Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X^{\varepsilon}_{s})
. Since the rescaled SLBP is a Markov process, then

(4.12)

F_{t}(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})-F_{0}(X^{\varepsilon}_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}\left(\partial_{1}+\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}\right)F(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s})ds,\quad t\geq 0,

where
\partial_{1}
is the derivative of
F
in its first coordinate, is a local martingale. We have

(4.13)

\partial_{1}F(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s})=\sum_{x^{\prime}}\partial_{s}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x^{\prime})Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X_{s}),

where

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\mathbb{G}_{t-s}^{\varepsilon}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n_{x}!}\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{z\in x}\partial_{s}G_{t-s}^{\varepsilon}(z,\sigma(z))\prod_{z^{\prime}\in x\backslash\{z\}}G^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(z^{\prime},\sigma(z^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n_{x}!}\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{z\in x}\frac{\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}}{2}G^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(\cdot,\sigma(z))(z)\prod_{z^{\prime}\in x\backslash\{z\}}G^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(z^{\prime},\sigma(z^{\prime})).

where
\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}
is the discrete Laplacian defined in (
4.10
). Plugging this expression into (
4.13
) and using the symmetry of
\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{1}F(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{\prime}}\partial_{s}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x^{\prime})Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X^{\varepsilon}_{s})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x^{\prime}}\sum_{z\in x}\frac{\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}}{2}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(\cdot,x^{\prime})(x)Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X^{\varepsilon}_{s})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x^{\prime}}\sum_{z\in x^{\prime}}\frac{\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{z}}{2}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(\cdot,x)(x^{\prime})Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X^{\varepsilon}_{s}),

where
\Delta_{z}^{\varepsilon}
the Laplacian acting on functions of configurations is defined in (
4.9
).
We now compute
\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{RW}F(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s})
to show that it cancels with
\partial_{1}F(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s})
. By (
B.10
) in Lemma
47
and the symmetry of
\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{RW}F(s,\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{-2}}{2}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}_{t-s}^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},x)\left\{Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})^{z,z+1})+Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})^{z,z-1})-2Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X^{\varepsilon}_{s})\right\}

(4.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}_{t-s}^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},x)\mathscr{G}_{RW}^{\varepsilon}Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x^{\prime}}\sum_{z\in x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}_{t-s}^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},x)\Delta_{z}^{\varepsilon}Q^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,X^{\varepsilon}_{s})(x^{\prime}).

By a simple change of variables, we obtain

\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{RW}F(s,\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})=\varepsilon^{-2}\sum_{x^{\prime}}\sum_{z\in x^{\prime}}\Delta_{z}^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{G}_{t-s}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,x)(x^{\prime})Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X^{\varepsilon}_{s})=-\partial_{1}F(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s}).

Going back to the local martingale (
4.12
), we see that only
\mathscr{G}_{BC}^{\varepsilon}F(s,\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})
remains under the integral sign, which proves that the process
\mathrm{M}^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot}(x)
defined in (
4.11
) is a local martingale. Since we are working with a fixed
\varepsilon
, the SLBP
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
is dominated by a rescaled pure birth process
\overline{\mathrm{X}}^{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\varepsilon^{\kappa}\overline{\mathrm{N}}^{\varepsilon}
, where
\overline{\mathrm{N}}^{\varepsilon}
is a pure birth process with transitions
n\mapsto n+k
at rate
np_{k}^{\varepsilon}
and started from
\overline{N}^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}N_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)
, where
\mathrm{N}^{\varepsilon}
is from Definition
3
. In particular, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|M_{s}^{\varepsilon}(x)|\right]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{s}^{\varepsilon})|\right]<\infty
for every
t\geq 0
. It then easily follows from the Vitali convergence theorem that
\mathrm{M}^{\varepsilon}(x)
is a martingale. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 26
.

In subsequent uses of the Markov property to obtain local martingales for a fixed
\varepsilon
, similar arguments to the ones used at the end of the proof of Lemma
25
will show that the local martingales are in fact martingales. We will therefore omit those details.

Next, we state a key lemma, which in particular provides an upper bound on the expectation of
\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}Q^{\varepsilon}(x,\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})
. The lemma and its proof are adapted from Proposition 2.1 in
[
Boldrighini et al., 1987
]
.

Lemma 27
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}Q^{\varepsilon}(x,\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})

(4.15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(x)}\Bigg\{q_{+}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\sum_{\ell\geq 1}p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\Big[Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)+\ell)-Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\Big]

\displaystyle-x(z)q_{-}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)-1}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)-1)\Bigg\}\prod_{z^{\prime}\in\operatorname{supp}(x)\backslash\{z\}}Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z^{\prime})}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z^{\prime})).

Moreover, define

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X^{\varepsilon}_{s})\right],\quad t>0,\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Then, for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
c=c_{k}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}Q^{\varepsilon}(x,\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\leq ck\varphi_{k-1}(t),

for all
t>0
and
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

For configurations
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}Q^{\varepsilon}(x,\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(x)}\Bigg\{q_{+}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\sum_{\ell\geq 1}p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\Big[Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)+\ell)-Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\Big]

\displaystyle+q_{-}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\Big[Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)-1)-Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\Big]\Bigg\}\prod_{z^{\prime}\in\operatorname{supp}(x)\backslash\{z\}}Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z^{\prime})}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z^{\prime})).

For any natural numbers
k\leq n
, we have

Q_{k}(n-1)-Q_{k}(n)=(n-1)(n-2)\cdots(n-k+1)[n-k-n]=-kQ_{k-1}(n-1).

Applying this observation to the competition term in the generator above, we obtain (
4.15
). Next, we observe that

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-\kappa}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\Big[Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)+\ell)-Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\Big]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{\kappa(n_{x}(z)-1)}\mu_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))^{n_{x}(z)-1}+R_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)),

where
R_{\varepsilon}
is a polynomial of degree strictly smaller than
n_{x}(z)-1
, and which in
\varepsilon
, satisfies

R_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))=O\left(\max_{j\in\{1,\cdots,n_{x}(z)-1\}}\varepsilon^{\kappa j}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\ell^{j}\right).

Since the offspring distribution is truncated at
L(\varepsilon)
, which satisfies

\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\varepsilon^{\kappa}L(\varepsilon)=0,

and we have
\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\ell p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}<\infty
by assumption, we obtain

\varepsilon^{\kappa j}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\ell^{j}\leq\varepsilon^{\kappa}(\varepsilon^{\kappa}L(\varepsilon))^{j-1}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\ell p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}=o(1),

for any choice of
j
. In particular, there exists an absolute constant
c_{1}>0
such that

\varepsilon^{\kappa(n_{x}(z)-1)}\Big\{\mu_{\varepsilon}n^{\kappa(n_{x}(z)-1)}+R_{\varepsilon}(n)\Big\}\leq c_{1}\varepsilon^{\kappa(n_{x}(z)-1)}n^{\kappa(n_{x}(z)-1)},

for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Moreover, there exists an absolute constant
c_{2}>0
such that

c_{1}n^{k-1}\leq c_{2}Q_{k-1}(n-1),

for all
k,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
k\leq n
. Thus

\displaystyle q_{+}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(x))\sum_{\ell\geq 1}p_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\Big[Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)+\ell)-Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-x(z)q_{-}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)-1}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)-1)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{\kappa}\Big\{c_{2}q_{+}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))-\varepsilon^{-\kappa}x(z)q_{-}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\Big\}Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)-1}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)).

We further note that

\varepsilon^{\kappa}\Big\{c_{2}q_{+}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))-\varepsilon^{-\kappa}x(z)q_{-}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\Big\}=c_{2}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)-x(z)X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa})/2

is bounded from above by some constant
c_{3}(x(z))>0
uniformly in
X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z)\geq 0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Hence for any
s>0
, we can upper bound the expectation of (
4.15
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}Q^{\varepsilon}(x,\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(x)}Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z)-1}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z))\prod_{z^{\prime}\in\operatorname{supp}(x)\backslash\{z\}}Q^{\varepsilon}_{n_{x}(z^{\prime})}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z^{\prime}))\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}k\varphi_{k-1}(s),

where
c_{3}=\max_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(x)}c_{3}(x(z))
. This proves the lemma.
∎

We apply the tools above to prove Proposition
24
.

Proof of Proposition
24
.

By Lemma
25
and Tonelli’s theorem, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X^{\varepsilon}_{t})\right]=\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\mathbb{E}\left[Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X^{\varepsilon}_{0})\right]+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x^{\prime})\mathbb{E}\left[\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},\cdot)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})\right]ds,

for all
t\geq 0
. We have
\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x^{\prime})=1
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Thus, by Lemma
27
and Assumption
A2
.a, there are constants
c_{0,k},c_{1,k}>0
such that

\varphi_{k}(t)\leq c_{0,k}+c_{1,k}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{k-1}(s)ds,\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}},\quad t\geq 0,

with
\varphi_{0}\equiv 1
. It follows by iteration on the right-hand side that there exist functions
h_{k}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
, for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that
\varphi_{k}(t)\leq h_{k}(t)<\infty
for all
t\geq 0
.
∎

We record a corollary to Proposition
24
which will be helpful in later sections.

Corollary 28
.

For any
T>0
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
p>1
, there exists
c=c(T,n,p)>0
such that

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X^{\varepsilon}_{t})^{p}\right]\leq c.

Proof.

Let
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. It is easy to see that
n^{m}\leq m^{m}Q_{m}(n)
for all
n\geq m
, and therefore

(4.16)

X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)^{m}\leq\varepsilon^{\kappa m}m^{m}+m^{m}Q_{m}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)),

for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
,
t\geq 0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. We can write the falling factorial as

(4.17)

Q_{k}(n)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}s(k,i)n^{i},\quad k\leq n,\quad k,n\in{\mathbb{N}},

where
s(k,i)
,
0\leq i\leq k
, denote the Stirling numbers of the first kind. Given a configuration
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\leq\prod_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(x)}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{x}(z)}s(n_{x}(z),i)\varepsilon^{\kappa(n_{x}(z)-i)}[\varepsilon^{\kappa i}i^{i}+i^{i}Q_{i}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z))].

Using this bound and Jensen’s inequality, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon})^{p}\right]
