uniformly in
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, for some
c>0
and
c_{1}\coloneqq\max(c,c_{0})
. By Assumption
A2
.a, there is
c_{2}>0
such that
v_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x^{1,z}+x^{1,z^{\prime}}|\nu^{\varepsilon})\leq c_{2}\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}
uniformly in
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, where
x^{1,z}=\varepsilon^{\kappa}\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\}}
. By Lemma
51
, there exists
C=C(T)>0
such that

(5.22)

|\varphi_{s,t}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon z)|<C,

uniformly over
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Thus overall for
A_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{1+2\gamma-\kappa}c_{1}\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\varphi_{0,t}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon z)^{2}+c_{2}\varepsilon^{1+2\gamma-\kappa}C^{2}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\leq(c_{1}+c_{2})C^{2},

where we have used that
1+2\gamma-\kappa=0
in the second step. Next, we apply (
5.8
) of Corollary
30
to control
A_{2}
. We obtain for some
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending only on
T
, the constant
c_{0}
above, and
C
of (
5.22
) that

A_{2}\leq c_{1}T^{2}+c_{2}\varepsilon^{4+2\gamma-2\kappa}.

It remains to control the martingale term
A_{3}
. Since for each
t\geq 0
,
{(M_{s}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot,t})^{2}-\langle M_{\cdot}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot,t})\rangle_{s})_{s\in[0,t)}}
is a martingale, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[M_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot,t})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\langle M_{\cdot}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot,t})\rangle_{t}\right]

(5.24)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t})\right]dr,

where we have used Fubini’s theorem in the last step, and
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
is defined in (
5.19
). By Proposition
24
, we obtain
A_{3}\leq c
for some
c>0
dependent on
T
and
c_{0}
. Gathering the estimates, we have shown that
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\varphi)^{2}\right]\leq c_{1}
, for some
c_{1}=c_{1}(T,c_{0},C)>0
. For
\mathbb{E}[Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\Delta^{\varepsilon}\varphi)^{2}]
and
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{t}\varphi)^{2}\right]
, we consider the problem (
5.16
) with terminal condition
\Delta^{\varepsilon}\varphi
and
\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{t}\varphi
, respectively. Then the arguments just presented for the first bound carry over. This proves the lemma.
∎

Using Markov’s inequality and the fact that
\varphi_{t,t}^{\varepsilon}=\varphi
, Lemma
33
, implies tightness of
\{Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\varphi):\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
in
\mathbb{R}
, for each
t\in[0,T]
. Next, we show the equicontinuity condition (
5.15
). Fix any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. Let
{\{\tau_{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}}
be a family of
[0,T]
-valued stopping times and let
{\{\theta_{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}\subset[0,\infty)}
be such that
\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\theta_{\varepsilon}=0
. Note in particular that
{\{\theta_{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}\subset[0,\overline{\theta}]}
, where
{\overline{\theta}\coloneqq\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\theta_{\varepsilon}\in[0,\infty)}
. By Markov’s inequality, we have

(5.25)

\mathbb{P}\left(|Y^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)-Y^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)|>\eta\right)\leq\eta^{-2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Y^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)-Y^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)\right)^{2}\right]

Then, the following lemma implies (
5.15
) the equicontinuity in probability.

Lemma 34
.

We have

\limsup_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Y^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)-Y^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)\right)^{2}\right]=0.

Proof.

By Lemmas
44
and
45
, the semimartingale decomposition of
\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}
is given by

Y_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)+\int_{0}^{t}\left(Y_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\Delta^{\varepsilon}\varphi)-F_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\right)dr+M_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi),\quad t\geq 0,

for some martingale
(M_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi))_{t\geq 0}
. Thus, we have

(5.26)

\displaystyle Y_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-Y_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}\left(Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\Delta^{\varepsilon}\varphi)-Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\widetilde{\rho}_{r}^{\>\varepsilon}\varphi)\right)dr

\displaystyle+\int_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}\left(Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\widetilde{\rho}_{r}^{\>\varepsilon}\varphi)-F_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\right)dr

\displaystyle+M^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)-M_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi),

Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Y_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-Y_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 3\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq t\leq T+\overline{\theta}\\
t-s\leq\theta_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\left(\int_{s}^{t}\left(Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\Delta^{\varepsilon}\varphi)-Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\widetilde{\rho}_{r}^{\>\varepsilon}\varphi)\right)dr\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+3\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq t\leq T+\overline{\theta}\\
t-s\leq\theta_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\left(\int_{s}^{t}\left(Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\widetilde{\rho}_{r}^{\>\varepsilon}\varphi)-F_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\right)dr\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+3\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)-M^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)\right)^{2}\right].

We control the terms on the right-hand side in turn. Denote them
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
, and
A_{3}
, respectively. By Jensen’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\theta_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T+\overline{\theta}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\Delta^{\varepsilon}\varphi)-Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\widetilde{\rho}_{r}^{\>\varepsilon}\varphi)\right)^{2}\right]dr

\displaystyle\leq 3\theta_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{T+\overline{\theta}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\Delta^{\varepsilon}\varphi)^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\widetilde{\rho}_{r}^{\>\varepsilon}\varphi)^{2}\right]\right)dr

By Lemma
33
, the first three expectations are bounded by a constant depending on
T
and on
\varphi
and the uniform bound on
\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}
from part two of Lemma
15
. By (
5.9
) of Corollary
30
, the next term,
A_{2}
, satisfies
A_{2}\leq c_{1}\theta_{\varepsilon}^{2}+c_{2}\varepsilon^{2+2\gamma-2\kappa}
for some
c_{1}=c_{1}(T,\varphi),c_{2}=c_{2}(T,\varphi)>0
. Finally, we control the martingale term
A_{3}
.
By the Itô isometry, we compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)-M^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}dM_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\varphi)dr\right].

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz and Jensen inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\varphi)dr\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\varphi)dr\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\theta_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\left(\int_{0}^{T+\overline{\theta}}\mathbb{E}\left[\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\varphi)^{2}\right]dr\right)^{1/2}

It follows from (
5.19
), Lemma
51
and Proposition
24
that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)-M^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}(\varphi)\right)^{2}\right]\leq c^{\prime}\theta_{\varepsilon}^{1/2},

for some
c^{\prime}
depending on
T
and (
5.22
). Thus for some
C>0
, we have
A_{3}\leq C\theta_{\varepsilon}^{1/4}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
31
.

The proposition follows from Lemma
33
, (
5.25
), Lemma
34
, and the proof outline given below Proposition
31
.
∎

5.3.
Identification of the limit

By Proposition
31
, any subsequence of
(\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}
has a further subsequence (which we still denote
(\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}
for simplicity) which converges in distribution in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
as
\varepsilon\searrow\infty
to a limit point
\widetilde{\mathrm{Y}}\in\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
. We establish the following proposition which implies that
\widetilde{\mathrm{Y}}
is the solution to (
1.2
).

Proposition 35
.

Let
\mathrm{Y}=(Y_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
denote the solution to the stochastic partial differential equation (
1.2
). Then, for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}\in[0,T]
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, we have

(5.27)

(\widetilde{Y}_{t_{1}}(\varphi),\cdots,\widetilde{Y}_{t_{k}}(\varphi))\overset{d}{=}(Y_{t_{1}}(\varphi),\cdots,Y_{t_{k}}(\varphi)).

To prove this result, we adapt the approach in section 4.5 of
[
Forien and Penington, 2017
]
. By Lemma
32
, we have the semimartingale decomposition

(5.28)

Y^{\varepsilon}_{s}(\varphi_{s,t}^{\varepsilon})=Y^{\varepsilon}_{0}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{0,t})+\int_{0}^{s}\left(Y_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{r}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t})-F_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t})\right)dr+M^{\varepsilon}_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon}),\quad t\geq 0,

for some martingale
(M^{\varepsilon}_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon}))_{s\in[0,t)}
with predictable quadratic variation

\langle M^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon})\rangle_{s}=\int_{0}^{s}\Gamma_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{r,t}^{\varepsilon})dr,

where
\Gamma_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{r,t}^{\varepsilon})
is given by (
5.19
). On the right-hand side of (
5.28
), we will show that the first term converges in distribution to a centred Gaussian variable by Assumption
A2
.a, that the martingale part converges in the Skorohod topology on
\mathscr{D}([0,t],\mathbb{R})
to a centred Gaussian martingale with time-
s
variance

Q_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t})\coloneqq\int_{0}^{s}\left(\langle(\varphi_{r,t})^{2},[(\sigma^{2}+\mu^{2})\rho_{r}+\rho_{r}^{2}/2]\rangle+\langle\left(\partial_{z}\varphi_{r,t}\right)^{2},\rho_{r}\rangle\right)dr,

for a suitable limiting
\varphi
, and that the integral term vanishes in
L^{2}
by Proposition
20
. The convergence of the finite-dimensional distributions claimed in Proposition
35
will then follow from standard arguments. In the following lemma, we prove the convergence of the martingale term.

Lemma 36
.

We have
(M_{s}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon}))_{s\in[0,t]}\overset{d}{\to}(M_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}))_{s\in[0,t]}
in the Skorohod topology on
\mathscr{D}([0,t],\mathbb{R})
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, where
(M_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}))_{s\in[0,t)}
is a centred Gaussian martingale with time-
s
variance given by
Q_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t})
, and
\varphi_{\cdot,t}
satisfies

(5.29)

\begin{cases}\partial_{s}\varphi_{s,t}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}\varphi_{s,t}(z)+(\mu-\rho_{s}(z))\varphi_{s,t}(z)=0,&s\in[0,t),\quad z\in\mathbb{S}^{1},\\
\varphi_{t,t}(z)=\varphi(z),&z\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
\Delta M_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon})\coloneqq M_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon})-M_{s-}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon})
. By
[
Jacod and Shiryaev, 2002
, Theorem 3.11, Chapter VIII]
, it suffices to show the following two points.

1.

Almost surely, we have
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{s\in[0,t)}|\Delta M^{\varepsilon}_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon})|<\infty
and
{\sup_{s\in[0,t)}|\Delta M^{\varepsilon}_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon})|\to 0}
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

2.

For each
s\in[0,t)
, we have
\langle M^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon})\rangle_{s}\to Q_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t})
in probability as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
.

Recall that the offspring distribution is truncated at
L(\varepsilon)=o(\varepsilon^{-\kappa})
. So we have almost surely that
\Delta M_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon})=Y_{s}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{s,t}^{\varepsilon})-Y_{s-}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{s-,t}^{\varepsilon})=O(L(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{1+\gamma-\kappa})=o(1)
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, uniformly in
s\in[0,t)
. This proves the first point. To prove the second point, we observe using (
5.18
), (
5.19
) and
{Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}=\varepsilon^{\kappa-\gamma}(X_{r}^{\varepsilon}-\mathbb{E}[X_{r}^{\varepsilon}])}
that
\langle M^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot}(\varphi_{\cdot,t}^{\varepsilon})\rangle_{s}=\int_{0}^{s}\Gamma_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{r,t}^{\varepsilon})dr
, where

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{\kappa-\gamma}\left[F_{r}^{\varepsilon}((\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t})^{2})-Y^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{r}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t})^{2})\right]+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon z)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)(X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa})\right]

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}(X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\mathbb{E}[X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)])\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{r}(\varepsilon z)(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon z))^{2}

\displaystyle+X_{r}^{\varepsilon}\left((\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon\cdot)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\big(\nabla^{\varepsilon,-}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon\cdot)\big)^{2}+\big(\nabla^{\varepsilon,+}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon\cdot)\big)^{2}\right]\right).

By Proposition
20
, the time integral of first term vanishes in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, and thus also in probability. By (
1.8
), (
1.6
), and Theorem
21
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)(X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa})\right]

\displaystyle=v^{\varepsilon}_{r}(2\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\}},\rho_{r}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})+2\rho_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)\mathbb{E}\left[X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)\right]-\rho_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)^{2}

\displaystyle=v^{\varepsilon}_{r}(2\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\}},\rho_{r}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})+2\rho_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)\left(v^{\varepsilon}_{r}(\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\}},\rho_{r}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})+\rho_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)\right)-\rho_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\varepsilon\searrow 0}{\sim}\rho_{r}(K_{\varepsilon}^{-1}z)^{2}.

Therefore, using Lemma
53
, we obtain the (deterministic) convergence

\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon z)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)(X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\varepsilon^{\kappa})\right]dr\overset{\varepsilon\searrow 0}{\to}\int_{0}^{s}\langle(\varphi_{r,t})^{2},\rho_{r}^{2}/2\rangle dr.

For the remaining two terms, we use the following observation. Suppose that
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}\in C(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is continuous in
r
, and suppose that
\sup_{0\leq r\leq t\leq T}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}-\psi_{r,t}\|_{\infty}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
for some limiting
\psi_{r,t}
. Then,

\int_{0}^{s}X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\psi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}-\psi_{r,t})dr\overset{\varepsilon\searrow 0}{\to}0,

in probability. Indeed, by the Jensen and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities, we have

\left|\int_{0}^{s}X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\psi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}-\psi_{r,t})dr\right|\leq\int_{0}^{s}dr\left(\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}(\psi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon z)-\psi_{r,t}(\varepsilon z))^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

By the (concave) Jensen inequality and Corollary
28
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)^{2}\right)^{1/2}\right]\leq\left(\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)^{2}\right]\right)^{1/2}<\infty,

uniformly in
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
r\in[0,T]
.
The claim then follows from Markov’s inequality and Lemma
53
. We conclude that

\int_{0}^{s}\varepsilon\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}(X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\mathbb{E}[X_{r}^{\varepsilon}(z)])\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{r}(\varepsilon z)(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon z))^{2}dr\overset{\varepsilon\searrow 0}{\to}0,

in probability, and that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}X_{r}^{\varepsilon}\left((\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{2})\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon\cdot)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\big(\nabla^{\varepsilon,-}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon\cdot)\big)^{2}+\big(\nabla^{\varepsilon,+}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t}(\varepsilon\cdot)\big)^{2}\right]\right)dr

\displaystyle\overset{\varepsilon\searrow 0}{\to}\int_{0}^{s}\left(\langle(\varphi_{r,t})^{2},[(\sigma^{2}+\mu^{2})\rho_{r}]\rangle+\langle\left(\partial_{z}\varphi_{r,t}\right)^{2},\rho_{r}\rangle\right)dr

in probability.
∎

Proof of Proposition
35
.

Fix
t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}\in[0,T]
, and apply (
5.28
) to write

(5.30)

\displaystyle(Y_{t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{0,t_{1}}),\cdots,Y_{t_{k}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{0,t_{k}}))

\displaystyle=(Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{0,t_{1}}-\varphi_{0,t_{1}}),\cdots,Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{0,t_{k}}-\varphi_{0,t_{k}}))

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\left(Y_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{r}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t_{1}})-F_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t_{1}})\right)dr,\cdots,\int_{0}^{t_{k}}\left(Y_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{r}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t_{k}})-F_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t_{k}})\right)dr\right)

\displaystyle+(M_{t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\cdot,t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}),\cdots,M_{t_{k}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\cdot,t_{k}}^{\varepsilon})).

Re-using the arguments that lead to (
5.23
), we obtain that
Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{0,t_{i}}-\varphi_{0,t_{i}})\to 0
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
for each
i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
. Therefore, the first vector on the right-hand side of (
5.30
) vanishes in probability as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. By Proposition
20
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\int_{0}^{t_{k}}\left(Y_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\>\varepsilon}_{r}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t_{k}})-F_{r}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{r,t_{k}})\right)dr\right)^{2}\to 0,

in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
, which shows that the second term on the right-hand side of (
5.30
) vanishes in probability. By Lemma
36
, the martingale term converges in distribution in path space. In particular, we have

(5.31)

(M_{t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\cdot,t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}),\cdots,M_{t_{k}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{\cdot,t_{k}}^{\varepsilon}))\overset{d}{\to}(M_{t_{1}}(\varphi_{\cdot,t_{1}}),\cdots,M_{t_{k}}(\varphi_{\cdot,t_{k}}))

as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
, and where the limiting centred Gaussian variables
M_{t_{i}}(\varphi_{\cdot,t_{i}})
have variance given by
Q_{s}(\varphi_{\cdot,t_{i}})
for each coordinate
i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
, and
\varphi_{\cdot,t_{i}}
solves (
5.29
) with
t=t_{i}
. The full convergence in distribution of the right-hand side of (
5.30
) to that of (
5.31
) follows from Slutsky’s Theorem.

By assumption,
\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}\overset{d}{\to}\widetilde{\mathrm{Y}}
converges in
\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}))
as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. Since the jumps of
\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}
are of magnitude
\varepsilon^{1+\gamma}L(\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon
, they vanish in the limit as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. By continuity of the map (see e.g.
[
Billingsley, 1999
, Example 12.1]
)

\mathscr{D}([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R},\quad\mathrm{Z}=(Z_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\mapsto\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|Z_{t}-Z_{t-}|,

we have
\widetilde{\mathrm{Y}}\in C([0,T],C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime})
. Thus, the finite-dimensional distributions of
\mathrm{Y}^{\varepsilon}
converge in distribution to those of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Y}}
. Then, by the continuous mapping theorem, we have

(Y_{t_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{0,t_{1}}),\cdots,Y_{t_{k}}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-Y_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{0,t_{k}}))\overset{d}{\to}(\widetilde{Y}_{t_{1}}(\varphi)-\widetilde{Y}_{0}(\varphi_{0,t_{1}}),\cdots,\widetilde{Y}_{t_{k}}(\varphi)-\widetilde{Y}_{0}(\varphi_{0,t_{k}})),

as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
. Furthermore, by Assumption
A2
.a, Lindeberg’s central limit theorem and the Cramér-Wold device, the initial data
\widetilde{Y}_{0}
is a
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})^{\prime}
-valued centred Gaussian variable with variance
\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Y}_{0}(\varphi)^{2}]=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\varphi(z)^{2}\rho_{0}(z)dz
for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. Using the arguments for the uniqueness of the mild solution in
[
Walsh, 1986
, Theorem 5.2]
, we obtain (
5.27
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
9
.

The theorem follows from Propositions
31
and
35
, and
[
Billingsley, 1999
, Theorem 13.1]
.
∎

6.
Proof of convergence of the
v
-functions

The aim of this section is to prove Theorem
21
by adapting the approach of
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
, Lemma 1]
. Let us briefly recall the notation and the statement of the result. We assume that the starting configuration
X_{0}^{\varepsilon}
of the rescaled SLBP satisfies Assumption
A2
. Recall from Definition
13
the
v
-functions
v^{\varepsilon}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\nu^{\varepsilon}}[V^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon};\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon})],\quad x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon},\quad t\geq 0,

\displaystyle V^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon};\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{x_{0}\preccurlyeq x}Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},X_{t}^{\varepsilon})(-1)^{n_{x}-n_{x_{0}}}\prod_{z\in x\backslash x_{0}}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z),\quad x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon},

and where
\rho^{\varepsilon}
is the discrete-space McKean representation (
C.16
). By Lemma
14
, the
v
-functions vanish if and only if
X_{t}^{\varepsilon}
is a product over
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
of independent Poisson distributions with intensities
\varepsilon^{-\kappa}\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)
and rescaled by
\varepsilon^{\kappa}
. Assume that the offspring distribution
\mathrm{P}
has a finite moment of order
p
, for some
p\in[1,2]
. Suppose also that
\kappa\in[0,3/8]
. If
p\in[1,2]
and
n>2
, we have

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})|=o(\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}),\quad\textit{as $\varepsilon\searrow 0$}.

If
p\in[1,2)
, then for all
n\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})|=o(\varepsilon^{1+(p-1)\kappa}),\quad\textit{as $\varepsilon\searrow 0$}.

If
p=2
and
n\in\{1,2\}
, there exists
c=c(T,p,n)>0
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})|\leq c\varepsilon^{1+\kappa},\quad\textit{for all $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$}.

The proof of Theorem
21
consists of three main steps: short-term estimates of the
v
-functions, smoothing properties of the semi-discrete FKPP equation, and an application of the Markov property. In Section
6.1
, we prove a series of lemmas which allows us to control the rescaled SLBP over short time intervals of length
\varepsilon^{\beta}
, for some
\beta>0
. We then show a smoothing property of the semi-discrete FKPP equation, namely that after a short amount of time, the solution started from a typical configuration of the rescaled SLBP stays close to that started from the smooth initial data
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}
of Definition
17
. Finally, we tie these results together using the Markov property to suitably restart
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}
and obtain the control claimed in Theorem
21
.

6.1.
Short-time control of the
v
-functions

Fix a time-horizon
T>0
and we let
t_{k}^{\varepsilon}=k\varepsilon^{\beta}
, for
k\in\{0,1,\cdots,\lfloor T/\varepsilon^{\beta}\rfloor\}
. In this section, we show that there exists an event
\Omega_{1}
of probability arbitrarily close to one, and such that if
\mathrm{X}=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}\in\Omega_{1}
is a path, then
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}=(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
started from
X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}
, for some
k
, remains close to the solution
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot}(\cdot;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})
to the semi-discrete FKPP equation (
1.10
) started from
X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}
, for small enough
t>0
. Since
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{1}
with high probability - see Lemma
38
below - this result shows that the dynamics of typical trajectories of the rescaled SLBP are close to those of the semi-discrete FKPP equation (
1.10
) at least for small times. The lemma is phrased in terms of the
v
-functions, and its proof follows that of
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
, Proposition 4.1]
.

Lemma 37
.

Fix
\kappa\geq 0
,
\beta\in(0,2)
,
\xi\in(0,\beta/2)
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
q>0
. Then, there exist an event
\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{1}(\xi,\beta,\kappa,n,q)
and a constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(T,n)>0
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{1})>1-c_{1}\varepsilon^{q}
and such that the following statements hold. Fix any path
\mathrm{X}=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}\in\Omega_{1}(\xi)
. There exists
c=c(T,n)>0
such that for all
\delta_{0}\in(0,(1-\beta/2)/2)
, we have

(6.1)

\sup_{t\in[\varepsilon^{\beta},2\varepsilon^{\beta}]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x|X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|<c\varepsilon^{\delta_{0}n},

for all
k\in\{0,1,\cdots,\lfloor T/\varepsilon^{\beta}\rfloor\}
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Moreover,
c(T,n^{\prime})>c(T,n)
for all natural numbers
n^{\prime}>n
. Furthermore, there exists
c=c(T,n)>0
such that

(6.2)

\sup_{t\in[0,\varepsilon^{\beta}]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x|\nu^{\varepsilon})|<c\varepsilon^{\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor(1+\kappa)},

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. We also have
c(T,n^{\prime})>c(T,n)
for all natural numbers
n^{\prime}>n
.

The proof of this result is divided into several lemmas on which we progressively establish short-time control on the
v
-functions. The first lemma controls, with high probability, two quantities of interest over a future time interval of length
\varepsilon^{\beta}
. It quantifies the growth (in
\varepsilon^{-1}
) of the solution to the semi-discrete FKPP equation (
1.10
) when started from a typical configuration of the rescaled SLBP configuration. It also bounds the expected growth rate of the polynomials
Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X^{\varepsilon}_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})
defined in (
1.10
), for test configurations
x
of a given size. The proof is very similar to that of
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
, Lemma 4.1]
.

Lemma 38
.

Fix any
\xi\in(0,\beta)
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
q>0
. Then, there exist an event
\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{1}(\xi,\beta,\kappa,n,q)
and a constant
c=c(T,n)>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{1})>1-c\varepsilon^{q}
, and for any
\mathrm{X}=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}\in\Omega_{1}
, the following inequalities hold: for any
k\in\{0,\cdots,\lfloor T/\varepsilon^{\beta}\rfloor\}
, there exists
c=c(T)>0
such that

(6.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}|\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|<c\varepsilon^{-\xi}

(6.4)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\varepsilon^{\beta}]}\max_{x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}:n_{x}=n}\mathbb{E}_{X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}}[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon})]<\varepsilon^{-\xi n},

where
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})
,
t\geq 0
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
is the solution to (
1.10
) started from
X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
24
, there exists
c(n,T)>0
such that

(6.5)

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon})\right]\leq c(n,T).

Let
\zeta\in(0,\xi)
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. By a union bound (recalling that there are
\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
sites in
\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
) and Markov’s inequality, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)>\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\right)\leq\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)^{m}\right]\varepsilon^{\zeta m}.

Observe that there exists
c_{1}=c_{1}(m)>0
such that
n^{m}\leq c_{1}Q_{m}(n)
for all natural numbers
n\geq m
. We consider
n=\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)
and we partition on
n<m
and
n\geq m
to obtain

X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)^{m}\leq\varepsilon^{\kappa m}m^{m}+c_{1}Q^{\varepsilon}_{m}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z))\leq m^{m}+c_{1}Q^{\varepsilon}_{m}(\varepsilon^{-\kappa}X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)).

Thus, by (
6.5
), we have

\displaystyle\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)^{m}\right]

\displaystyle\leq m^{m}+c_{1}c(m,T).

It follows that

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)>\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\right)\leq c_{2}\varepsilon^{\zeta m-1},

uniformly in
k\in\{0,1,\cdots,\lfloor T/\varepsilon^{\beta}\rfloor\}
, and where
c_{2}(m,T)=m^{m}+c_{1}(m)c(m,T)
. Next, define the event

\overline{\Omega}=\left\{\mathrm{X}\in\mathscr{D}([0,T],\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}):\max_{k\in\{0,1,\cdots,\lfloor T/\varepsilon^{\beta}\rfloor\}}\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}X_{t^{\varepsilon}_{k}}(z)\leq\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\right\}.

We have

\overline{\Omega}^{c}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{\lfloor T/\varepsilon^{\beta}\rfloor}\left\{\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}(z)>\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\right\}.

Hence, choosing
m
sufficiently large for
\zeta m>2+\beta+q
to hold, we obtain by a union bound that

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\overline{\Omega}^{c}\right)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor T/\varepsilon^{\beta}\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}X^{\varepsilon}_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}(z)>\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\right)=Tc_{2}(m,T)\varepsilon^{\zeta m-2-\beta}<Tc_{2}(m,T)\varepsilon^{q},

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Consequently,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\overline{\Omega}\right)=1-\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\overline{\Omega}^{c}\right)\geq 1-Tc_{2}(m,T)\varepsilon^{q}.

On
\overline{\Omega}
, the control of
\rho^{\varepsilon}
claimed in (
6.3
) follows from part two of Lemma
15
. It remains to control
{\mathbb{E}_{X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}}[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon})]}
. By Lemma
25
, for any
t\in[0,\varepsilon^{\beta}]
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}}[Q^{\varepsilon}(x,X_{t}^{\varepsilon})]=\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x^{\prime})\mathbb{E}_{X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}}[\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X_{s}^{\varepsilon})]ds,

where
x^{\prime}
ranges over configurations
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
n_{x^{\prime}}=n_{x}=n
. To bound the first term, we use that
n_{x^{\prime}}=n
and
\mathrm{X}\in\overline{\Omega}
to obtain

\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})\leq\varepsilon^{-\zeta n}\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=\varepsilon^{-\zeta n}.

where the last equality holds since
\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=1
. In absolute value, the second term is upper bounded by

R^{\varepsilon}(x)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{\beta}}\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x^{\prime})\left|\mathbb{E}_{X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}}\left[\mathscr{G}^{\varepsilon}_{BC}Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X^{\varepsilon}_{s})\right]\right|ds.

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
. It is easy to see using Corollary
28
that for some constant
c(n,N,T)>0
, we have

\max_{x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}:n_{x}=n}\mathbb{E}\left[R^{\varepsilon}(x)^{N}\right]\leq c(n,N,T)\varepsilon^{\beta N},

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Then, a union bound and Markov’s inequality imply that

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}:n_{x}=n}R^{\varepsilon}(x)>1\right)\leq c(n,N,T)\varepsilon^{\beta N}{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor\choose n}\leq c^{\prime}(n,N,T)\varepsilon^{\beta N-n}

for some
c^{\prime}(n,N,T)>0
. We introduce

\Omega_{1}\coloneqq\overline{\Omega}\cap\left\{\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}R^{\varepsilon}(x)\leq 1\right\}.

By a union bound, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{1}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\overline{\Omega}^{c}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}:n_{x}=n}R^{\varepsilon}(x)>1\right)

\displaystyle<Tc_{1}(m)c(m,T)\varepsilon^{q}+c^{\prime}(n,N,T)\varepsilon^{\beta N-n}.

We may now choose
N
large enough that
\beta N>n+q
, which leads to
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{1})>1-c\varepsilon^{q}
, for some constant
c=c(n,T,m,N)>0
. Since
\zeta\in(0,\xi)
, this proves (
6.4
) as long as
\mathrm{X}\in\Omega_{1}
.
∎

In the following technical lemma, we find an upper bound on the
v
-functions by iterating the hierarchy of equations from Proposition
22
, and by using the properties of paths which belong to the event
\Omega_{1}
of Lemma
38
.

Lemma 39
.

Consider
\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{1}(\xi,\beta,\kappa,n,q)
the event identified in Lemma
38
. Fix a path
\mathrm{X}=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}\in\Omega_{1}
, and introduce the space
\mathbf{T}_{i}(t)=\{(s_{i-1},\cdots,s_{1},s_{0}):0\leq s_{i-1}\leq\cdots\leq s_{1}\leq s_{0}=t\}
of ordered times. Let

m:\mathbb{Z}\to{\mathbb{N}}
be given by

m(h)\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&h=1\\
\max(2,-h)&h\leq 0\end{cases},

and for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
t\geq 0
, define maps
a^{\varepsilon}:\mathbf{T}_{2}(t)\times\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}}\to\mathbb{R}
by

a^{\varepsilon}_{s_{1},t}(x,z_{1},h_{1})f\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbbm{1}_{n_{x_{1}}(z_{1})\geq m(h_{1})}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s_{1}}(x,x_{1})f\left(x_{1}^{(z_{1},h_{1})}\right),

for all
(s_{1},t)\in\mathbf{T}_{2}(t)
,
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
,
z_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
,
h_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
f\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}}
, where
x_{1}^{(z_{1},h_{1})}
denotes the configuration
x_{1}
with
h_{1}
particles added at
z_{1}
if
h_{1}\geq 0
, and
-h_{1}
particles removed from
z_{1}
if
h_{1}<0
, and
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot}(\cdot;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})
is the solution to the semi-discrete FKPP equation (
1.10
) started from
X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}
which is constructed in (
C.16
). We also introduce for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
t\geq 0
, and
i\geq 0
, maps
{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}:\mathbf{T}_{i+1}(t)\times\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}^{i}\times\mathbb{Z}^{i}\to\mathbb{R}}
given by

(6.6)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{t,x,x_{i+1}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i};z_{1},\cdots,z_{i};h_{1},\cdots,h_{i})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\coloneqq a_{s_{1},t}^{\varepsilon}(x,z_{1},h_{1})a_{s_{2},s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,z_{2},h_{2})\cdots a_{s_{i},s_{i-1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,z_{i},h_{i})\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{s_{i}}(\cdot,x_{i+1})\mathbbm{1}_{n_{\cdot}=n_{x_{i+1}}},

for all
(s_{i},\cdots,s_{1},s_{0}=t)\in\mathbf{T}_{i+1}(t)
,
x,x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
,
z_{1},\cdots,z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, and
h_{1},\cdots,h_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, for any
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
n_{x}=n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exist constants
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,N),c_{2}=c_{2}(n)>0
such that

(6.7)

\displaystyle|v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}|X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\varepsilon^{\left[\beta-\xi(1+n/(N+1))\right](N+1)}+C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle+c_{2}\varepsilon^{-\xi N(n+N)}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1},\cdots z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\\
h_{1},\cdots,h_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{\kappa\lceil n_{x_{i+1}}/2\rceil-\xi\lfloor n_{x_{i+1}}/2\rfloor}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{t}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}ds_{2}\cdots\int_{0}^{s_{i-1}}ds_{i}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{t,x,x_{i+1}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i};z_{1},\cdots,z_{i};h_{1},\cdots,h_{i}),

for all
t\geq 0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

For each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we define maps
A^{\varepsilon}:\mathbf{T}_{2}(t)\times\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}}\to\mathbb{R}
by

A^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}(x)f\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,x_{1})\sum_{z_{1}\in\operatorname{supp}(x_{1})}\sum_{h_{1}=-n_{x_{1}}(z_{1})}^{1}c_{h}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1}(z_{1}),\rho_{s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}))f\left(x_{1}^{(z_{1},h_{1})}\right),

for all
(s,t)\in\mathbf{T}_{2}(t)
,
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
,
f\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}}
, where
c_{h}^{\varepsilon}
are the coefficients derived in Proposition
22
,
x_{1}^{(z_{1},h_{1})}
denotes the configuration
x_{1}
with
h_{1}
particles added at
z_{1}
if
h_{1}\geq 0
, and
-h_{1}
particles removed from
z_{1}
if
h_{1}<0
, and
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot}(\cdot;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})
is the solution to the semi-discrete FKPP equation (
1.10
) started from
X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}
which is constructed in (
C.16
). We also introduce for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
maps
C_{i}^{\varepsilon}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i\geq 0
, defined by

(6.8)

C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x_{1})V^{\varepsilon}(x_{1},X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}};X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}),\quad t\geq 0,\quad x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon},

and

C_{i}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}ds_{2}\cdots\int_{0}^{s_{i-1}}ds_{i}A_{s_{1},t}^{\varepsilon}(x)A_{s_{2},s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)\cdots A_{s_{i},s_{i-1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(s_{i},\cdot),\quad i\geq 1,

with
s_{0}=t
. Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and define for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
the map
R_{N+1}^{\varepsilon}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
by

R_{N+1}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}ds_{2}\cdots\int_{0}^{s_{N}}ds_{N+1}A_{s_{1},t}^{\varepsilon}(x)A_{s_{2},s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)\cdots A_{s_{N+1},s_{N}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)v_{s_{N+1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\rho_{s_{N+1}}|X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}).

By Proposition
22
, the
v
-functions solve the following hierarchy of equations

\displaystyle v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot\;;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x_{1})V^{\varepsilon}(x_{1},X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}};X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})

(6.9)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\int_{0}^{t}ds_{1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s_{1}}(x,x_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\sum_{z_{1}\in\operatorname{supp}(x_{1})}\sum_{h_{1}=-|x_{1}(z_{1})|}^{1}c_{h}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1}(z_{1}),\rho_{s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(z_{1};X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}))v_{s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1}^{(z_{1},h_{1})},\rho_{s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}),

for all
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
,
t\geq 0
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. The same equation is satisfied by
v_{s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}
. Thus, by iterating (
6.9
)
N
times, we may write

v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}|X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})=\sum_{i=0}^{N}C_{i}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)+R_{N+1}^{\varepsilon}(t,x).

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We claim that there exists
c_{1}(n,N)>0
such that

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=n\end{subarray}}|R_{N}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)|<c_{1}\varepsilon^{[\beta-\xi(1+2n/N)]N}.

Since
\mathrm{X}\in\Omega_{1}
and
n_{x_{N}}+h_{N}\leq n+N
, we apply Lemma
38
to obtain

\displaystyle|v_{s_{N}}^{\varepsilon}(x_{N}^{(z_{N},h_{N})},\rho^{\varepsilon}_{s_{N}}(\cdot;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\sum_{x^{\prime}\preccurlyeq x_{N}^{(z_{N},h_{N})}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}}[Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X_{s_{N}}^{\varepsilon})](-1)^{n_{x_{N}}+h_{N}-n_{x^{\prime}}}\prod_{z\in x_{N}^{(z_{N},h_{N})}\backslash x^{\prime}}\rho_{s_{N}}^{\varepsilon}(z)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x^{\prime}\preccurlyeq x_{N}^{(z_{N},h_{N})}}\mathbb{E}_{X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}}[Q^{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},X_{s_{N}}^{\varepsilon})]\varepsilon^{-\xi(n_{x_{N}}+h_{N})}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n+N}\varepsilon^{-\xi(1+2n/N)N},

which proves the claim with
c_{1}(n,N)=2^{n+N}
. We interchange the order of summation in each
A_{s_{i},s_{i-1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{i}}=n_{x_{i-1}}+h_{i-1}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{s_{i-1}-s_{i}}(x_{i-1}^{(z_{i-1},h_{i-1})},x_{i})\sum_{z_{i}\in\operatorname{supp}(x_{i})}\sum_{h_{i}=-n_{x_{i}}(z_{i})}^{1}c_{h_{i}}^{\varepsilon}(x_{i}(z_{i}),\rho_{s_{i}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\sum_{h_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{i}}=n_{x_{i-1}}+h_{i-1}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{s_{i-1}-s_{i}}(x_{i-1}^{(z_{i-1},h_{i-1})},x_{i})\mathbbm{1}_{n_{x_{i}}(z_{i})\geq m(h_{i})}c^{\varepsilon}_{h_{i}}(x_{i}(z_{i}),\rho_{s_{i}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})).

The threshold in the indicator function ensures that births can only occur at locations where there is at least one existing particle, and deaths can only occur at locations with two or more particles; the same constraints apply in the dynamics of the SLBP. Observe that for each
j\in\{0,1,\cdots,i\}
, we have
n_{x_{j}}(z_{j})\leq n+j
since at most one particle is added to the test configuration during each interaction. Then, by Proposition
22
and Lemma
38
, we have for some constant
c_{0}(n)>0

(6.10)

c^{\varepsilon}_{h_{j}}(x_{j}(z_{j}),\rho_{s_{j}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}))\leq c_{0}(n)\varepsilon^{-\xi(n_{x_{j}}(z_{j})+1)}\leq c_{0}(n)\varepsilon^{-\xi(n+j+1)},

for each
j\in\{1,\cdots,i\}
. Hence the integrand of
C_{i}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
satisfies

\displaystyle A_{s_{1},t}^{\varepsilon}(x)A_{s_{2},s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)\cdots A_{s_{i},s_{i-1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(s_{i},\cdot)

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}(n)\varepsilon^{-\xi i(n+i)}a_{s_{1},t}^{\varepsilon}(x,z_{1},h_{1})a_{s_{2},s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,z_{2},h_{2})\cdots a_{s_{i},s_{i-1}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,z_{i},h_{i})C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(s_{i},\cdot),

for some other constant
c_{0}(n)>0
. At most one particle is added to the test configuration during each interaction, so
n_{x_{i+1}}\leq n+i
. The second point of Lemma
46
then implies that

\displaystyle|V^{\varepsilon}(x_{i+1},X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}};X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|

\displaystyle<c^{\prime}(n)\varepsilon^{\kappa\lceil n_{x_{i+1}}/2\rceil}\left(\max_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(x_{i+1})}X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}(z)\right)^{\lfloor n_{x_{i+1}}/2\rfloor}

(6.11)

\displaystyle<c^{\prime}(n)\varepsilon^{\kappa\lceil n_{x_{i+1}}/2\rceil-\xi\lfloor n_{x_{i+1}}/2\rfloor},

for some
c^{\prime}(n)>0
. Combining the calculations above shows (
6.7
) with
c_{2}(n)=c_{0}(n)c^{\prime}(n)
.
∎

A key step in the proof of Lemma
37
is to show that the term involving
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
in (
6.7
) is small with respect to
\varepsilon
. The method used to show the analogous bound (4.13) in Appendix B of
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
applies to our setting with the following added difficulty. The (deterministic) dynamics of the
v
-functions computed in Proposition
22
can result in the killing of the entire local population in a single interaction. This is directly caused by the unboundedness of the offspring distribution
\mathrm{P}
. By contrast, in the binary branching case in
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
, it is not possible to kill the entire local population in the dynamics of the
v
-functions as long as the local population size is larger than a certain fixed threshold. In the main step of the argument, one simplifies the problem of controlling
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
by first bounding the trajectories of particles which are eventually killed. We resolve the added difficulty by carefully recording the impact of this first step on the initial configuration. Finally, there is also one aspect of the method which is simpler in our setting, namely the fact that our rates of birth and competition are linear and quadratic, respectively, as opposed to falling factorials of arbitrary degrees. This simplifies Lemma
41
below.

Lemma 40
.

Assuming that
\beta\in(0,2)
, there exists
c=c(T,n)>0
such that

(6.12)

\int_{0}^{2\varepsilon^{\beta}}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}ds_{2}\cdots\int_{0}^{s_{i-1}}ds_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1},\cdots z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{t,x,x_{i+1}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i};z_{1},\cdots,z_{i};h_{1},\cdots,h_{i})\leq c\varepsilon^{\beta i+(1-\beta/2)n/2},

for all
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
such that
n_{x}=n
,
h_{1},\cdots,h_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

Before proving this lemma, we follow Appendix B of
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
and introduce a graphical representation of
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
.

The function
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
defined in (
6.6
) describes the (deterministic) evolution of a configuration
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
of
n
particles backwards in time from
t>0
to time zero. The particles undergo successive periods of diffusive motion, separated by instantaneous reactions in which at some location
z
at some time
s
, a number of particles
h
is added (if
h\in\{0,1\}
) or removed (if
h<0
). During the interactions, particles can only be added at
z
if there were at least particle present at time
s+
, and particles can only be removed if there were at least two particles at time
s+
. At most one particle is added locally, while the entire local population may be removed.

We represent
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
as a directed graph with vertices at
(s_{i},z_{i})
, where the
s_{i}
are interaction times (with
s_{0}=t
the initial time since we work backwards in time), and the
z_{i}
are the respective locations of the interactions. If there are multiple particles at the same location
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
at the initial time
t>0
, they each get assigned their own vertex, still denoted
(t,z)
. This is to account for the fact that their subsequent diffusive motion is independent. For each pair of vertices
(s_{i},z_{i})
and
(s_{j},z_{j})
,
s_{i}>s_{j}
, we add an edge
\{(s_{i},z_{i}),(s_{j},z_{j})\}
, directed backwards in time, and corresponding to the factor
G^{\varepsilon}_{s_{i}-s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})
(i.e. to the random motion of a given particle from
z_{i}
to
z_{j}
in time
s_{i}-s_{j}
). At a given reaction time
s_{i}+<t
which is not the initial time, particles which are about to interact non-trivially at
z_{i}
meet at the same vertex
(s_{i},z_{i})
, and interact according to
h_{i}
. In the following lemma, we identify as in
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
four types of vertices which are sufficient to describe any such interaction.

Lemma 41
.

Given a vertex
(s,z)
, we denote by
m
the number of incoming edges, and by
p
the number of outgoing edges. We can encode
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
of (
6.6
) as a (directed) graph by the above construction using only the following four types of vertices :

T1.

No interaction:
h=0
, so
m=1
,
p=1
;

T2.

Birth of one particle:
h=1
, so
m=1
,
p=2
;

T3.

Death of one particle:
h=-1
, so
m=2
,
p=1
;

T4.

Death of
-h\geq 2
particles:
h\leq-2
, so
m=-h
,
p=0
.

Proof.

We note that in the dynamics described by
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
and generally by (
6.9
), any local sub-colony of particles may vanish during an interaction as long as there are two particles present before the interaction. If there are
m\geq 2
incoming edges at vertex
(s,z)
and
h\in\{-1,\cdots,-m\}
, then we can describe the event that
|h|
of the
m
particles vanish in terms of the vertices of type T1-T4. Indeed, if
m\geq 2
and
h\not=-1
when
m=2
, then we decompose the interaction into a T4 vertex where
|h|
of the
m
particles meet and vanish, and
m-|h|
T1 vertices, where particles just perform diffusion and interact trivially. When
m=2
and
h=-1
, this construction is not allowed since it would require a vertex in which a particle self-annihilates. Instead, we simply use a T3 vertex. If
m=1
, trivial interactions (
h=0
) are captured by T1 vertices, and births, which in
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
can at most add one particle locally (
h=1
) are directly captured by T2 vertices. If
m\geq 2
and
h=0
, we simply let
m
particles have their own T1 vertex at
(s,z)
. If
m\geq 2
and
h=1
, then we let
m-1
particles have their own vertex at at
(s,z)
, while one particle has a T2 vertex at
(s,z)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
40
.

We may assume without loss of generality that the undirected version of the graph just constructed is connected. Indeed, if there are more than one connected components, the integrand on the left-hand side of (
6.12
) splits as a product over the connect components.

We prove the estimate (
6.12
) using the following two steps as in
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
. We first use basic properties of the Green’s function (the semigroup property,
\sum_{x^{\prime}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=1
, and the estimate (
C.2
)) to prune the graph until we have reduced the sub-graph which excludes the final (time-
0
) vertices to a binary tree. This has the effect of greatly simplifying the expression for
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
, and leaves us with the task of showing (
6.12
) when only T2 vertices, i.e. terms of the form
G^{\varepsilon}_{s_{k}-s_{i}}(x_{i},x_{k})G^{\varepsilon}_{s_{k}-s_{j}}(x_{j},x_{k})
, are present in its graphical representation. While carrying out this pruning, we keep track of the maximum number of initial (time-
2\varepsilon^{\beta}
) particles which are removed, and of the resulting contributions to an upper bound on the left-hand side of (
6.12
). In a second step, we control the remaining binary tree using (
C.2
) and the key observation that all final vertices have at least two incoming edges, since otherwise
V^{\varepsilon}(x_{i+1},X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}};X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})=0
by Lemma
46
.

Before starting the pruning procedure, we let
I_{4}
denote the number of particles in the initial configuration
x
which die at a
T4
vertex before reaching time zero. We also denote by
T_{i}
the number of T
i
vertices for
i\in\{1,\cdots,4\}
.

We first remove the vertices of type T1 using the semigroup property. Indeed, if
(s_{k},z_{k})
is of type T1 with incoming edge
\{(s_{i},z_{i}),(s_{k},z_{k})\}
and outgoing edge
\{(s_{k},z_{k}),(s_{j},z_{j})\}
, then

\sum_{z_{k}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}}G^{\varepsilon}_{s_{i}-s_{k}}(z_{i},z_{k})G^{\varepsilon}_{s_{k}-s_{j}}(z_{k},z_{j})=G^{\varepsilon}_{s_{i}-s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j}),

so we remove
(s_{k},z_{k})
,
\{(s_{i},z_{i}),(s_{k},z_{k})\}
, and
\{(s_{k},z_{k}),(s_{j},z_{j})\}
from the graph and we add an edge
\{(s_{i},z_{i}),(s_{j},z_{j})\}
. This step does not cause the removal of any initial particle. Next, we collapse the T3 vertices into T1 vertices, starting with the smallest times, and using the Green’ function estimate (
C.2
). Each time the estimate (
C.2
) is applied to remove an edge, we need to worry about the vertex at the other end of the edge. If it is an initial vertex, we remove it from the initial configuration
x
. If it is a vertex of type T2 , then it only has two out-edges (we are in a special case of Appendix B of
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
since our birth rates are linear in the local population size), and it therefore becomes a T1 vertex. If it is a T3 vertex, it becomes a T4 vertex. By Lemma
41
, T3 vertices have two incoming edges, so the new T4 vertex also has two incoming edges. For future reference, denote by
T_{4}^{+}
the total number of additional T4 vertices created in this way. Since each T3 vertex has two incoming edges, we have overall that the number
I_{3}\coloneqq c_{3}n_{x}
, for some
c_{3}\in[0,1]
, of initial vertices that are removed by the above procedure satisfies
I_{3}\leq\lfloor(n_{x}-I_{4})/2\rfloor
.

Next, we control all the T4 vertices (the
T_{4}
original ones, as well as the
T_{4}^{+}
newly created ones), again in order of increasing time coordinate. By Lemma
41
, a T4 vertex always has at least two incoming edges, so that
2T_{4}\leq I_{4}
. At a given T4 vertex with
k
incoming edges, we apply (
C.2
)
k-1
times to remove all but one of the edges, and then we use
\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{s}(z,z^{\prime})=1
to control the remaining edge. The other end of the removed edges is handled as above, and we apply the semigroup property to control any remaining T1 vertices. Overall, if
I_{4}=c_{4}n_{x}
, for some
c_{4}\in[0,1]
, the number of particles removed from the initial configuration
x
by controlling the
T_{4}
original T4 vertices is
I_{4}-T_{4}=c_{4}n_{x}-T_{4}\geq c_{4}n_{x}/2
. We have also controlled
T_{4}^{+}\leq\lfloor(n_{x}-I_{4})/2\rfloor-I_{3}
new T4 vertices created. Each of these has exactly two incoming edges, hence if
I_{4}^{+}\coloneqq c_{4}^{+}n_{x}
, some
c_{4}^{+}\in[0,1]
denotes the number of initial particles removed in the process, then
I_{4}^{+}\leq T_{4}^{+}\leq\lfloor(n_{x}-I_{4})/2\rfloor-I_{3}
.

Let us summarise what we have shown so far. Let
K\coloneqq T_{1}+T_{3}+T_{4}+T_{4}^{+}\in\{0,1,\cdots,i\}
be the number of vertices of
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
that we have just pruned. The above algorithm constructs a sub-configuration
x^{\prime}
of
x
and a simplified object

\overline{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}=\overline{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}_{t,x^{\prime},x_{i+1}}(s_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,s_{k}^{\prime};z_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,z_{k}^{\prime};\underbrace{1,\cdots,1}_{k\textnormal{-times}}),

where
k\in\{0,1,\cdots,i-K\}
is the number of remaining vertices, which are all of type T2, and if
k=0
the particles diffuse without interacting. The (directed) graph representation of
\overline{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon}
for all times larger than zero is a binary tree. Let
I\coloneqq I_{3}+I_{4}^{+}+I_{4}-T_{4}\geq(c_{3}+c_{4}^{+}+c_{4}/2)n_{x}
denote the number of initial particles removed while reducing
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
to
\overline{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
. Using the bounds on
I_{3}
,
I_{4}^{+}
, and
I_{4}
above, we compute

(6.13)

n_{x^{\prime}}=n_{x}-I\geq(1-c_{4})n_{x}/2+T_{4}.

Let
J\geq I
be the total number of factors of the form (
C.2
) picked up to bound T3 and T4 vertices. Then we have also shown that there exists is
c_{0}=c_{0}(T,n)>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1},\cdots z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{t,x,x_{i+1}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i};z_{1},\cdots,z_{i};h_{1},\cdots,h_{i})

(6.14)

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\varepsilon^{J}\prod_{j=1}^{J}(s_{i_{j}-r_{j}}-s_{i_{j}})^{-1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1}^{\prime},\cdots z_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\overline{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}_{t,x^{\prime},x_{i+1}}(s_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,s_{k}^{\prime};z_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,z_{k}^{\prime};1,\cdots,1),

where
s_{i_{j}}
,
j\in\{1,\cdots,J\}
are the time coordinates of the T3 and T4 vertices that we have just removed, and
s_{i_{j}-r_{j}}
are the time coordinates of their incoming edges, with
1\leq r_{j}\leq i_{j}\leq i
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

We now implement the second step of the proof, in which we find an upper bound for the right-hand side of (
6.14
) using (
C.2
) and the fact if the final configuration
x_{i+1}
is not zero, then each site has at least two incoming edges. We may assume without loss of generality that
\overline{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
is connected. We say that a vertex is in the bulk (of the graph) if it is neither an initial nor a final vertex. We distinguish three types of edges in the graphical representation of
\overline{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
.

E1.

Edge with no interaction:
\{(t,z),(0,z^{\prime})\}
, for some
z\in x^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}\in x_{i+1}
.

E2.

Edge from initial to bulk:
\{(t,z),(s_{i},z_{i})\}
, for some
s_{i}\in(0,t)
,
z_{i}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
and
z\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
.

E3.

Edge from bulk to the final configuration:
\{(s_{i},z_{i}),(0,z^{\prime})\}
, for some
s_{i}\in(0,t)
,
z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, and
z^{\prime}\in x_{i+1}
.

Since
\overline{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}
is connected, either it is comprised entirely of a single final vertex with only type E1 incoming edges, or it has no final vertex with only type E1 incoming edges. In the first case, by (
6.13
), there are
n_{x^{\prime}}\geq n_{x}-I
such edges, so the worst bound is obtained when
x_{i+1}
consists of
\lfloor(n_{x^{\prime}}+1)/2\rfloor
sites each with two incoming E1 edges. For each final site, if the two initial particles are at
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
and two E1 edges carry them to their final location
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, we have by (
C.2
)

\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}G^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z_{1},z)G^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z_{2},z)\leq C\varepsilon/\sqrt{2\varepsilon^{\beta}}\leq C\varepsilon^{1-\beta/2},

where
C=C(T)>0
. Overall, in this case, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,z_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\overline{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}_{t,x^{\prime},x_{i+1}}(s_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,s_{k}^{\prime};z_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,z_{k}^{\prime};1,\cdots,1)\leq C\varepsilon^{(1-\beta/2)\lfloor(n_{x^{\prime}}+1)/2\rfloor},

for some
C=C(T)>0
. Combined with (
6.14
), we obtain

(6.15)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1},\cdots z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{t,x,x_{i+1}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i};z_{1},\cdots,z_{i};h_{1},\cdots,h_{i})

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\varepsilon^{J+(1-\beta/2)\lfloor(n_{x^{\prime}}+1)/2\rfloor}\prod_{j=1}^{J}(s_{i_{j}-r_{j}}-s_{i_{j}})^{-1/2},

uniformly in
h_{1},\cdots,h_{i}
, and where by construction the times in the first product do not intersect with the ones in the second product.

Suppose now that there are no final vertices whose incoming edges are all of type E1. Since any final vertex has at least two incoming edges, each one has at least one E3 edge. We use that edge to bound the summation over the final location by one. The other edges may be of type E1 or E3. We bound all of them using (
C.2
): an E1 edge is controlled by
C\varepsilon^{1-\beta/2}
as above, while an E3 edge is controlled by
C\varepsilon/\sqrt{s_{l_{q}}^{\prime}}
, if
s_{l_{q}}^{\prime}>0
is the time of its parent vertex in the bulk, where
l_{q}\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
,
q\in\{1,\cdots,\overline{k}\}
, for some
\overline{k}\in\{0,1,\cdots,k\}
representing the number of E3 edges we control in this way. Let
\overline{K}\in\{0,1,\cdots,n_{x^{\prime}}\}
denote the number of E1 edges in
\overline{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon}
. By construction, we have
\overline{k}+\overline{K}\geq\lfloor(n_{x^{\prime}}+1)/2\rfloor
. Therefore, there exists
c_{1}=c_{1}(T,n)>0
such that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,z_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\overline{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon}_{t,x^{\prime},x_{i+1}}(s_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,s_{k}^{\prime};z_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,z_{k}^{\prime};1,\cdots,1)\leq c_{1}\varepsilon^{(1-\beta/2)\overline{K}}\varepsilon^{\overline{k}}\prod_{q=1}^{\overline{k}}(s_{l_{q}}^{\prime})^{-1/2}.

With (
6.14
), this implies that

(6.16)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1},\cdots z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{t,x,x_{i+1}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i};z_{1},\cdots,z_{i};h_{1},\cdots,h_{i})

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\varepsilon^{J+\overline{k}+(1-\beta/2)\overline{K}}\prod_{j=1}^{J}(s_{i_{j}-r_{j}}-s_{i_{j}})^{-1/2}\prod_{q=1}^{\overline{k}}(s_{l_{q}}^{\prime})^{-1/2},

uniformly in
h_{1},\cdots,h_{i}
, and where by construction the times in the first product do not intersect with the ones in the second product. Our next step is to integrate (
6.15
) and (
6.16
) with the integration bounds specified in (
6.12
) in the statement of the lemma. We may extend all the upper integration bounds to
2\varepsilon^{\beta}
by working with
|s_{i_{j}-r_{j}}-s_{i_{j}}|^{-1/2}
on the right-hand side in either case. Then performing the integration of the second product in (
6.16
) gives a bound
2^{3\overline{k}/2}\varepsilon^{\overline{k}\beta/2}
. Similarly, integrating over the first product in both (
6.15
) and (
6.16
), starting with the
s_{i_{j}}
, we get a contribution of
4^{J}\varepsilon^{J\beta/2}
. For (
6.15
), we are left with
i-J
integrals on
(0,2\varepsilon^{\beta})
with constant integrand, leading to a contribution of
2^{i-J}\varepsilon^{(i-J)\beta}
, and to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\varepsilon^{\beta}}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}ds_{2}\cdots\int_{0}^{s_{i-1}}ds_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1},\cdots z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{t,x,x_{i+1}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i};z_{1},\cdots,z_{i};h_{1},\cdots,h_{i})

\displaystyle\leq c\varepsilon^{\beta i+(J+\lfloor(n_{x^{\prime}}+1)/2\rfloor)(1-\beta/2)},

for some
c=c(T,n)>0
. For (
6.16
), we are left with
{i-J-\overline{k}}
integrals on
(0,2\varepsilon^{\beta})
with constant integrand, which contribute
2^{i-J-\overline{k}}\varepsilon^{(i-J-\overline{k})\beta}
. Hence, for some
c=c(T,n)>0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\varepsilon^{\beta}}ds_{1}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}ds_{2}\cdots\int_{0}^{s_{i-1}}ds_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i+1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
z_{1},\cdots z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{t,x,x_{i+1}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i};z_{1},\cdots,z_{i};h_{1},\cdots,h_{i})

\displaystyle\leq c\varepsilon^{J+\overline{k}+(1-\beta/2)\overline{K}+\beta(i-J/2-\overline{k}/2)}=c\varepsilon^{\beta i+(J+\overline{K}+\overline{k})(1-\beta/2)}\leq c\varepsilon^{\beta i+(J+\lfloor(n_{x^{\prime}}+1)/2\rfloor)(1-\beta/2)}.

It remains to recall (
6.13
), and the fact that
J\geq I\geq 0
. Then,

J+\lfloor(n_{x^{\prime}}+1)/2\rfloor\geq I+\frac{n_{x^{\prime}}}{2}\geq\frac{n_{x}}{2}+\frac{I}{2}\geq\frac{n_{x}}{2},

and assuming that
\beta\in(0,2)
, this concludes the proof of (
6.12
).
∎

We can now prove Lemma
37
starting from the bound established in (
6.7
) of Lemma
39
.

Proof of Lemma
37
.

By Lemmas
39
and
40
, assuming
\beta\in(0,2)
and
\xi\in(0,\beta/2)
, there exist
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,N)>0
and
c_{2}=c_{2}(n,N)>0
such that

|v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}|X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|\leq c_{1}\varepsilon^{[\beta-\xi(1+n/(N+1))](N+1)}+C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)+c_{2}\varepsilon^{(1-\beta/2)n/2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{\beta i},

for all
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
n_{x}=n
,
t\in[\varepsilon^{\beta},2\varepsilon^{\beta}]
. We bound
C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)
, defined in (
6.8
), using Lemma
46
. If
x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}
has a location
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
with a single particle, then we split the
V
-function as a product and we see that the factor corresponding to
z
vanishes. Thus, if it contributes non-trivially,
x_{1}
necessarily has at least two particles at every location on its support. Using (
C.2
) and
t>\varepsilon^{\beta}
, the term
\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{\varepsilon^{\beta}}(x,x_{1})
is dominated by a product of
\lfloor(n_{x}+1)/2\rfloor\geq n/2
factors of
C\varepsilon^{1-\beta/2}
for some constant
C=C(T)>0
, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. On the other hand, by part two of Lemma
46
and the fact that
\mathrm{X}\in\Omega_{1}
, we have

|V^{\varepsilon}(x_{1},X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}};X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|\leq\varepsilon^{\kappa\lceil n/2\rceil-\xi\lfloor n/2\rfloor},

for some absolute constant
c>0
. Since
\xi\in(0,\beta/2)
is arbitrarily small and
\kappa\geq 0
, it follows that for some
c=c(T)>0
, we have for any
\delta_{0}\in(0,(1-\beta/2)/2)
that

(6.17)

C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\leq c\varepsilon^{\delta_{0}n},

Assume now that
\xi<\beta/2
, and choose
N=N(\beta,n)
sufficiently large that
\beta[1-(1+n/(N+1))/2](N+1)-\beta n/4\geq(1-\beta/2)n/2
. Along with the observation that
\sum_{i=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{\beta i}=(1-\varepsilon^{\beta(N+1)})/(1-\varepsilon^{\beta N})<2
, we obtain for some
C=C(T)>0
the bound

|v^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot\;;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})|\leq C\varepsilon^{(1-\beta/2)n/2},

for all
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
n_{x}=n
,
t\in[\varepsilon^{\beta},2\varepsilon^{\beta}]
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, which concludes the proof of (
6.1
). To show (
6.2
), we repeat the arguments above with the following modifications. By Proposition
22
, we have

\displaystyle v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x_{1})v_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1},\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})

(6.18)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}ds_{1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t-s_{1}}(x,x_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\sum_{z_{1}\in\operatorname{supp}(x_{1})}\sum_{h_{1}=-n_{x_{1}}(z_{1})}^{1}c_{h}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1}(z_{1}),\rho_{s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(z_{1}))v_{s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1}^{(z_{1},h_{1})},\rho_{s_{1}}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon}),

for all
t\in[0,\varepsilon^{\beta}]
and
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
n_{x}=n
.
Thus, in the proof of Lemma
39
, the initial term
C_{0}^{\varepsilon}
is now

C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x_{1}}=n_{x}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{G}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x,x_{1})v_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x_{1},\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon}).

By Assumption
A2
.a, it satisfies the inequality
|C_{0}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)|\leq c_{1,n}\varepsilon^{\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor(1+\kappa)}
uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
with
n_{x}=n
, for some constant
c_{1,n}>0
. Still in the proof of Lemma
39
, the new coefficients
c_{h_{j}}^{\varepsilon}
in the iteration of (
6.18
) are controlled using (
2.3
) and part two of Lemma
15
. Indeed, there is an absolute constant
C>1
such that
\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}|\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)|<C
, and there exists
c=c(n)>0
such that

(6.19)

|c_{h}^{\varepsilon}(x_{j}(z_{j}),\rho_{s_{j}}^{\varepsilon}(z_{j}))|\leq c\left(\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}|\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)|+1\right)^{n_{x_{1}}(z_{1})+1}\leq cC^{n+j+1},

where we have used in the last inequality that
n_{x_{j}}\leq n+j
. By Proposition
24
, the integrand of the new remainder
|R_{N+1}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)|
is bounded by a constant, uniformly in
\varepsilon
,
t
,
s_{1},\cdots,s_{N+1}
, and
x
. Thus we obtain the uniform bound
R_{N+1}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\leq c\varepsilon^{\beta(N+1)}
for some constant
c=c(T,n)>0
. The function
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
is unchanged and can therefore be controlled by Lemma
40
. The power of
\varepsilon
which multiplies
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
in (
6.7
) changes to
\varepsilon^{\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor(1+\kappa)}
since the bounds (
6.10
) and (
6.11
) are replaced with (
6.19
) and
(A2)
.
Overall, we obtain for some
c_{2}=c_{2}(T,n)>0
the bound

\displaystyle|v_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x,\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})|

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\varepsilon^{\beta(N+1)}+c_{2}\varepsilon^{\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor(1+\kappa)}\sum_{i=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{\beta i}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\varepsilon^{\beta(N+1)}+2c_{2}\varepsilon^{\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor(1+\kappa)}.

For
N=N(\delta,n)
large enough that
\beta(N+1)>\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor(1+\kappa)
, this shows (
6.2
).
∎

6.2.
A smoothing argument

To establish Theorem
21
, which quantifies in terms of
\varepsilon
the error in the approximation
X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\approx\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
and
t\in[0,T]
, we need one more result. We have shown in Lemma
37
that with high probability, we have control on the error in

X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\approx\rho_{t-t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z;X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}),

for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
,
t\in[t_{k+1}^{\varepsilon},t_{k+2}^{\varepsilon}]
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. In particular, the dynamics of the rescaled SLBP are close to those of the semi-discrete FKPP equation (
1.10
) for short-times. By iterating the last displayed expression, we have the heuristic

\displaystyle X_{t_{1}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)

\displaystyle\approx\rho_{t_{1}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z;X_{0}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle X_{t_{2}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)

\displaystyle\approx\rho_{t_{1}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z;\rho_{t_{1}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot;X_{0}^{\varepsilon}))

\displaystyle X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z)

\displaystyle\approx\underbrace{\rho_{t_{1}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z;\rho_{t_{1}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot;\cdots\rho_{t_{1}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot;X_{0}^{\varepsilon})))}_{k-times}\approx\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}(z).

We now show that with high probability, we have error bounds on

\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t-t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}(z;X^{\varepsilon}_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})\approx\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon},\quad t\in[t_{k+1}^{\varepsilon},T].

At time
t_{k}^{\varepsilon}
, the left-hand side is
X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}
, the time-
t_{k}^{\varepsilon}
configuration of a rescaled SLBP, while the right-hand side is
\rho_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}
. This result shows that if
X_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}
is close to
\rho_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}
, then the semi-discrete FKPP at any later time and started from either one takes on approximately the same value. This is a very similar statement to the continuity of solution or flow maps of well-posed differential equations. More precisely, we have the following statement, whose proof is based on that of Proposition 4.2 in
[
Boldrighini et al., 1992
]
.

Lemma 42
.

Assume that
\kappa\geq 0
,
\beta\in(0,6/15)
, and
\xi\in(0,\beta/2)
. Let
\delta_{*}\coloneqq\min(3/8+\beta/16,1/2-\beta/4)
. For any
\delta_{1}\in(0,\delta_{*}-\beta)
and
q>0
, there exist an event
\Omega_{2}=\Omega_{2}(\xi,\beta,\kappa,n,q)\subset\Omega_{1}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{2})>1-c\varepsilon^{q}
for some
c=c(T,p)>0
and a constant
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(T,\beta)\in(0,1)
such that

(6.20)

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in[t_{k+1}^{\varepsilon},T]}\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}|\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t-t_{k}^{\varepsilon}}(z;X^{\varepsilon}_{t_{k}^{\varepsilon}})-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)|<\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}}\Big|\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{2}\right)=1,

for all
k\in\{0,1,\cdots,\lfloor T/\varepsilon^{\beta}\rfloor\}
, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
.

Proof.

For
t\geq 0
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, we define

\Delta_{0}^{\varepsilon}(t,z)=\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z;X^{\varepsilon}_{0})-\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z),

and

\hat{X}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z)=X_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)-\mathbb{E}[X^{\varepsilon}_{0}(z)].

Since
\rho^{\varepsilon}
solves (
1.10
), we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}^{\varepsilon}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}G^{\varepsilon}_{t}(z,z_{1})\hat{X}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z_{1})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}ds\sum_{z_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}G^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(z,z_{1})[(\mu_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{s}^{\varepsilon}(z))\Delta_{0}^{\varepsilon}(s,z_{1})-\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{0}^{\varepsilon}(s,z_{1}))^{2}].

We begin by finding a bound for the absolute value of the integral on the right-hand side. Let

m_{0}=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}[X_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)],

which is finite by Definition
17
. By part two of Lemma
15
, there is
C=C(T)>0
such that
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\leq(m_{0}+1)C
for all
t\geq 0
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Suppose that
\mathrm{X}^{\varepsilon}\in\Omega_{1}
. Then using the triangle inequality,
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, and the fact transition probabilities sum to one, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{z_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}G^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(z,z_{1})[(\mu_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{s}^{\varepsilon}(z))\Delta_{0}^{\varepsilon}(s,z_{1})-\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{0}^{\varepsilon}(s,z_{1}))^{2}]\right|

\displaystyle\leq(\mu_{\varepsilon}+(m_{0}+1)C)(\varepsilon^{-\xi}+(m_{0}+1)C)+\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon^{-2\xi}+(m_{0}+1)^{2}C^{2}),

for all
s\in[0,t)
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
c_{0}=\mu_{\varepsilon}(1+C)+(1+C^{2})/2
, so that the right-hand side of the last displayed expression is dominated by
c_{0}\varepsilon^{-2\xi}(m_{0}+1)^{2}
, since
m_{0}>0
. We are interested in
t\geq t_{1}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{\beta}
but let us first assume that
t\geq\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\varepsilon^{(1-\beta/2)/2}
. Take
t=\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}
. We have

(6.21)

\left|\int_{0}^{\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}}ds\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}G^{\varepsilon}_{t-s}(x,y)[(\mu-\rho_{s}^{\varepsilon}(x))\Delta_{0}^{\varepsilon}(s,y)-\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{0}^{\varepsilon}(s,y))^{2}]\right|\leq c_{0}\varepsilon^{((1-\beta/2)/2-2\xi}(m_{0}+1)^{2}.

Next, we estimate the term

F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)\coloneqq\sum_{z_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}G_{\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z,z_{1})\hat{X}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z_{1}).

We will apply Chebyshev’s inequality to show that with high probability, we can control
F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)
uniformly in
z
. Observe that

\mathbb{E}\left[(F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z))^{N}\right]=\sum_{z_{1},\cdots,z_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}G_{\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z,z_{i})\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{N}\hat{X}^{\varepsilon}_{0}(z_{j})\right].

Given a function
\rho:\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}\to[0,\infty)
, define
\tilde{X}_{t}^{\varepsilon}=X_{t}^{\varepsilon}-\rho
, for all
t\geq 0
. For
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
a configuration, we let
\hat{x}\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
be defined by
\hat{x}(z)=x(z)\mathbbm{1}_{n_{x}(z)\geq 2}
. By (
B.2
) in Lemma
46
, we have

(6.22)

\prod_{z\in x}\tilde{X}_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)=\sum_{x_{1}\preccurlyeq\hat{x}}d^{\varepsilon}(\hat{x},x_{1};\rho)V^{\varepsilon}(x\backslash x_{1},X^{\varepsilon}_{0};\rho),

for all
x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}
, for some coefficients
d^{\varepsilon}(\hat{x},x_{1};\rho)
satisfying

(6.23)

d^{\varepsilon}(\hat{x},0;\rho)=1,\quad|d^{\varepsilon}(\hat{x},x_{1};\rho)|\leq c\left(\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}}\rho(z)\right)^{n_{x_{1}}},

for some constant
c=c(n_{\hat{x}},n_{x_{1}})>0
. We apply this formula with the function
\rho(z)=\mathbb{E}[X_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)]=\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z)
where the second equality is an assumption on our initial data - see Definition
17
. We obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z))^{N}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=N\end{subarray}}\prod_{z^{\prime}\in x}G_{\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})\sum_{x_{1}\preccurlyeq\hat{x}}d^{\varepsilon}(\hat{x},x_{1};\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})\mathbb{E}\left[V^{\varepsilon}(x\backslash x_{1},X^{\varepsilon}_{0};\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=N\end{subarray}}\prod_{z^{\prime}\in x}G_{\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})\sum_{x_{1}\preccurlyeq\hat{x}}d^{\varepsilon}(\hat{x},x_{1};\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})v_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x\backslash x_{1},\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon}).

We partition the first summation over the size of
\hat{x}
and split the product of transition probabilities over
\hat{x}
and
x\backslash\hat{x}
:

\mathbb{E}[(F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z))^{2N}]=\sum_{r=0}^{2N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{X}_{\varepsilon}\\
n_{x}=N\\
n_{\hat{x}}=r\end{subarray}}\sum_{x_{1}\preccurlyeq\hat{x}}d^{\varepsilon}(\hat{x},x_{1};\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})\left(\prod_{z^{\prime}\in x\backslash\hat{x}}G_{\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})\right)\left(\prod_{z^{\prime}\in\hat{x}}G_{\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})\right)v_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x\backslash x_{1},\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon});

the even exponent
2N
ensures positivity of the integrand on the left-hand side. By (
C.2
), there exists
C=C(T)>0
such that

G_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z,z^{\prime})\leq C\varepsilon/\sqrt{t},

for all
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\varepsilon}
,
t\in(0,T]
, and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. By definition, each factor in the product over
z^{\prime}\in\hat{x}
is repeated at least twice, meaning that we can extract at least
\lfloor(r+1)/2\rfloor
factors of
C\varepsilon/\sqrt{\underline{t}^{\varepsilon}}
to obtain an upper bound on that product. Using the bound on
d^{\varepsilon}
from (
6.23
), and the uniform bound
\rho_{t}^{\varepsilon}(z)\leq(m_{0}+1)C(T)
, we obtain

|d^{\varepsilon}(x,x_{1};\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})|\leq cC(T)^{2N}(m_{0}+1)^{2N},

for some absolute constant
c>0
, uniformly in
x
and
x_{1}\preccurlyeq x
. By Assumption
A2
.a and the fact that
(1-\beta/2)/2\in(2/5,1/2)
, there exist
c_{1,N}>0
such that

(6.24)

v^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x\backslash x_{1},\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|\nu^{\varepsilon})\leq c_{1,N}\varepsilon^{\lfloor(2N-n_{x_{1}}+1)/2\rfloor(1+\kappa)}<c_{1,N}\varepsilon^{(2N-n_{x_{1}})(1-\beta/2)/2}\leq c_{1,N}\varepsilon^{(2N-r)\delta_{0}},

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
x_{1}\preccurlyeq\hat{x}
, and
\delta_{0}\in(0,(1-\beta/2)/2)
(see Remark
43
below). The bounds just discussed imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z))^{2N}]

\displaystyle\leq cC(T)^{2N}(m_{0}+1)^{2N}c_{1,n}\sum_{r=0}^{2N}\varepsilon^{r(1-(1-\beta/2)/4)/2}\varepsilon^{(2N-r)\delta_{0}}.

The exponents simplify as follows

\displaystyle r(1-(1-\beta/2)/4)/2+(2N-r)\delta_{0}

\displaystyle=r(3/8+\beta/16)+(2N-r)\delta_{0}

\displaystyle\geq 2N\delta_{*}.

where
\delta_{*}=\min(3/8+\beta/16,\delta_{0})
. Hence, for some
C=C(T,N)>0
, we have

(6.25)

\mathbb{E}[(F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(z))^{2N}]\leq(m_{0}+1)^{2N}C\varepsilon^{2N\delta_{*}}

uniformly in
